7 Fault Tolerance

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Fleet stack focus for this chapter.

Why does scale transform hardware failure from rare exception to routine condition that systems must absorb continuously?

A single accelerator can run for years between hardware failures. A thousand accelerators turn that same component risk into failures every couple of days, and ten thousand see failures every few hours, while shared power and network faults can interrupt many workers at once. Individual devices need not become less reliable for the fleet to experience failures routinely. A program on one machine can often stop and restart when a component fails; a distributed training job must also recover agreement among surviving workers about which progress remains valid. Restarting quickly is of little value if the restored state silently changes the computation. Preserving more state reduces the work at risk, but consumes storage bandwidth and memory while the job is healthy. Waiting longer between saves reduces that overhead while increasing the progress a failure can erase. Serving imposes a different consequence: users may abandon a request before recovery completes, and replacing a worker may leave the request’s context unavailable. A response that arrives on time can still be unacceptable if recovery has discarded information needed to produce it. Engineers must therefore bound lost work and recovery delay while preserving the validity of training and the usefulness of service. The goal is continued useful execution despite repeated failures, with the costs and limits of recovery made explicit. In C³ terms, fault tolerance spends compute and communication on coordination so that component failures do not routinely become failures of the entire workload.

Learning Objectives
  • Calculate mean time between fleet failures from component rates and estimate interruption frequency for large training clusters
  • Classify hardware, software, silent-data-corruption, and slow-but-alive failures by detectability, blast radius, and recovery path
  • Select checkpoint intervals that balance write overhead against acceptable lost work and recovery delay
  • Design distributed checkpointing and elastic recovery plans for multi-terabyte model state across accelerator fleets
  • Design serving redundancy, failover, and state-recovery policies under latency budgets and partial failures
  • Select graceful degradation strategies that preserve useful service when models, features, regions, or capacity fail
  • Evaluate detection timeouts, restart strategies, and fault-injection evidence against a per-failure recovery-time budget
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

7.1 Failure Analysis at Scale

Imagine a 10,000-GPU cluster midway through a three-month training run for a new foundation model. The parallel training plan and the communication layer beneath it have done their job, and thousands of devices exchange gradients through AllReduce, AllGather, and AllToAll as if they were one machine. Then the arithmetic of scale catches up. A GPU fails every few hours, and if the system cannot absorb that ordinary physical event, synchronized training halts while compute worth about $0.5M a day sits idle. Fault tolerance is the system property that lets distributed execution continue making useful progress when components fail, degrade, or disappear. In the fleet stack of The Fleet Stack, fault tolerance occupies the Reliability & Control row of the Distribution band, where it protects the progress that parallelism and communication produce.

That fragility is the direct consequence of successful synchronization. Every collective in Collective Communication assumed that all ranks in its process group arrive, and each dependent consumer waits for every contribution. One stalled device can therefore stop progress in its synchronous training group, even while unrelated jobs continue elsewhere in the fleet. Fault-tolerant ML systems preserve progress by detecting failure, checkpointing recoverable state, restarting or elastically reshaping jobs, and treating hardware churn as normal execution rather than an exceptional path. That reliability envelope covers both halves of the fleet’s work. Training needs recovery that preserves weeks of accumulated progress, while serving needs redundancy, failover, and graceful degradation that preserve a latency contract, and both draw their spare capacity from a shared fleet pool.

↪ How the scheduler holds spare capacity for failed jobs and prepositions replacement nodes while a missed heartbeat is confirmed is developed in Failure as a scheduling input.

Not every failure announces itself. A component can stop outright, slow down while still answering liveness checks, or keep running while it computes wrong values. The quiet cases pair low detection rates with a high blast radius, because the system keeps running on state it should no longer trust. The recovery target here is therefore trustworthy execution: preserving valid training progress and restoring a service after its infrastructure or software fails. A restored model can still make poor predictions on changed inputs even when every component executes correctly. Evaluating that behavioral robustness requires a separate contract for model quality under distribution shifts and adversarial inputs, developed in Robust AI. The distinction lets us use model-quality signals to diagnose a fault without treating every quality regression as evidence of failed hardware.

Because failures cannot be eliminated at this scale, fault-tolerant systems verify completion rather than assume it, treat failure as a normal code path, exercise recovery continuously, and account for partial failures that naive error handling may not anticipate. These techniques draw on decades of distributed systems research, but ML workloads change the economics. Training checkpoints are large but can be sized from the model and optimizer state, while some optimization algorithms tolerate bounded numerical perturbations without requiring bitwise replay. That tolerance must be established for the training method; stochasticity does not make arbitrary corruption or inconsistent recovery safe. A recovery design can exploit approximate execution only while preserving its declared convergence and quality requirements. Making failure a routine part of operation starts with knowing how often failures arrive, and the mathematics that follows makes the shift from rare exception to routine occurrence precise.

Fleet mean time between failures (MTBF) collapses from 50,000 h to 5 h as GPUs multiply.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A production cluster operates 10,000 GPUs, where each individual GPU exhibits an independent MTTF of 50,000 hours and an availability of 99.99%. What is the resulting GPU-only cluster MTBF, and what is the probability that all 10,000 GPUs are simultaneously operational at any random instant?

    1. Cluster MTBF is 50 hours; simultaneous cluster availability is approximately 90.5%.
    2. Cluster MTBF is 500 hours; simultaneous cluster availability is approximately 99.0%.
    3. Cluster MTBF is 5.0 hours; simultaneous cluster availability is approximately 36.8%.
    4. Cluster MTBF is 0.5 hours; simultaneous cluster availability is approximately 1.0%.

  2. Using an illustrative unprotected-memory soft-error rate of 250 FIT/Mbit and 80 GiB of HBM per device, explain why a 1,024-GPU training cluster needs ECC protection.

See Answers →

7.2 The Mathematics of Inevitable Failure

A single data-center GPU can run for years without a hardware fault, yet a synchronous job spanning ten thousand of those same GPUs is interrupted every few hours. System reliability engineering explains why individual device longevity erodes so quickly when thousands of components must stay healthy at the same time (Birolini 2017). Each component has a failure rate \(\ComponentFailureRate\),1 measured in failures per unit time. When \(\ComponentFailureRate\) is constant, the probability that a single component survives without failure until time \(\TimeInstant\) follows an exponential distribution,2 as in equation 1:

1 Failure rate (\(\ComponentFailureRate\)): Expressed in FITs (Failures In Time), where 1 FIT equals one failure per billion device-hours. A data center GPU with a 50,000-hour mean time to failure has \(\text{FIT} = 20{,}000\), corresponding to \(\HourlyComponentFailureRate = 2.0 \times 10^{-5}\) failures/hour. That seems negligible for one device but becomes dominant at fleet scale: a cluster with 10,000 GPUs accumulates 200,000,000 FITs from GPUs alone, translating to an expected GPU failure every 5 hours.

2 Poisson process: A statistical model for events occurring independently at a constant average rate. In this model, event counts follow a Poisson distribution and waiting times follow an exponential distribution, giving the survival function \(\ComponentReliability(\TimeInstant) = e^{-\ComponentFailureRate \TimeInstant}\). With independent identical component hazards, the probability of at least one failure in a 10,000-component cluster is \(1 - e^{-\RequiredComponentCount\ComponentFailureRate \TimeInstant}\). The aggregate hazard \(\RequiredComponentCount\ComponentFailureRate\) scales linearly with component count; the failure probability is nonlinear in both \(\RequiredComponentCount\) and \(\TimeInstant\), approaching \(\RequiredComponentCount\ComponentFailureRate \TimeInstant\) only when that product is small.

\[ \ComponentReliability(\TimeInstant) = e^{-\ComponentFailureRate \TimeInstant} \tag{1}\]

↪ The independence, exponential-lifetime, and Poisson results this failure arithmetic rests on are developed in Probability tools for failure analysis.

3 GPU MTTF variation: This chapter uses 50,000 hours as a planning constant for current data center GPUs (A100 and H100 class), not as a guarantee for every deployed GPU. Published fleet studies from Google TPUv4 pods and Meta GPU clusters report that field reliability depends on topology, cooling, workload, and operational practice (Zu et al. 2024; Kokolis et al. 2025; Dubey et al. 2024). The planning constant therefore anchors the arithmetic, while operators should substitute measured fleet rates when they have them.

Zu, Y., A. Ghaffarkhah, H.-V. Dang, B. Towles, S. Hand, S. Huda, A. Bello, et al. 2024. “Resiliency at Scale: Managing Google’s TPUv4 Machine Learning Supercomputer.” 21st USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI 24), 761–74.
Kokolis, Apostolos, Michael Kuchnik, John Hoffman, Adithya Kumar, Parth Malani, Faye Ma, Zach DeVito, Shubho Sengupta, Kalyan Saladi, and Carole-Jean Wu. 2025. “Revisiting Reliability in Large-Scale Machine Learning Research Clusters.” 2025 IEEE International Symposium on High Performance Computer Architecture (HPCA), 1259–74. https://doi.org/10.1109/hpca61900.2025.00096.
Dubey, Abhimanyu, Abhinav Jauhri, Abhinav Pandey, Abhishek Kadian, et al. 2024. The Llama 3 Herd of Models. arXiv preprint arXiv:2407.21783.

The expected time to failure of this component equals \(1/\ComponentFailureRate\). In this book’s convention, mean time to failure (MTTF) names a single component’s expected time to its first failure. MTBF names the repairable system’s expected operating time between successive failures, excluding recovery downtime. Under the constant-rate exponential model used here the two coincide numerically, and the fault-tolerance model uses MTTF for per-component constants and MTBF for composed system rates. Data center GPUs often use planning MTTF values in the tens of thousands of hours, with field behavior depending on operating conditions, cooling effectiveness, manufacturing variation, and workload stress.3 Component failure rates catalogs the reference per-component FIT and MTTF values (GPU, high-bandwidth memory (HBM), network interface card (NIC), power supply, PCIe switch, optical cable, ToR switch) that anchor these \(\ComponentFailureRate\) and FIT figures. A reader can substitute the constant for any device and reproduce the rates used throughout the fault-tolerance analysis.

When multiple independent components operate in a system where any single failure causes system failure, equation 2 formalizes how system reliability becomes the product of individual component reliabilities:

\[ \SystemReliability(\TimeInstant) = \prod_{i=1}^{\RequiredComponentCount} {\ComponentReliability}_i(\TimeInstant) = \prod_{i=1}^{\RequiredComponentCount} e^{-\ComponentFailureRate_i \TimeInstant} \tag{2}\]

For \(\RequiredComponentCount\) identical components with individual failure rate \(\ComponentFailureRate\), equation 3 gives the identical-component simplification:

\[ \SystemReliability(\TimeInstant) = e^{-\RequiredComponentCount\ComponentFailureRate \TimeInstant} \tag{3}\]

The system failure rate becomes \(\RequiredComponentCount\ComponentFailureRate\), and equation 4 expresses how the system MTBF scales inversely with component count. This inverse scaling explains why per-device reliability that looks excellent in isolation erodes at cluster scale:

\[ \SystemMtbf = \frac{1}{\RequiredComponentCount\ComponentFailureRate} = \frac{\ComponentMttf}{\RequiredComponentCount} \tag{4}\]

The consequence is a gap between failure cadence and job length. At 10,000 GPUs, the GPU-only calculation gives an expected interval of 5 hours, so a training run measured in weeks accumulates dozens of expected failures and one measured in months accumulates hundreds. These are planning averages, not a schedule of incidents; a shorter job can finish without failure even when its expected failure count exceeds one. Once the expected time between failures falls below the length of the job, fault tolerance becomes part of the execution budget for long-running fleet work. Applied to the landmark training runs of The Scale Moment, whose accelerator counts grew by about four orders of magnitude between 2012 and 2024, the same per-device planning rate puts the largest of them, with more than ten thousand GPUs, below a 10-hour system MTBF. Recovery therefore becomes an active scheduling constraint rather than a reliability feature.

7.2.1 Composing component reliability

The GPU-only calculation of equation 4 supplies the scale intuition; a real training system then composes GPUs, HBM, links, power supplies, storage paths, and redundancy into one failure budget. Component rates are usually quoted in the FIT units introduced with the failure rate, so the 50,000-hour GPU contributes 20,000 FIT to whatever system contains it. The new question is how different component rates compose, and the composition rule depends on the redundancy structure and the independence assumptions. For series systems (for example, a node where all 8 GPUs must work), failure of any component fails the whole. With independent constant-rate failures, the reliabilities multiply as in equation 2, so the rates add even when the components differ: \[\SystemMtbf = \frac{1}{\sum \ComponentFailureRate_i}\] Under this additive failure-rate law, the system failure rate grows linearly with component count while system MTBF falls in inverse proportion. Every unredundanted component on the critical path adds its FIT contribution directly to the denominator.

The same product governs instantaneous availability, a different quantity from the survival function \(\ComponentReliability(\TimeInstant)\). Availability \(\SteadyStateAvailability\) is the probability that a component is up at a randomly chosen instant, counting repairs, whereas \(\ComponentReliability(\TimeInstant)\) is the probability that it has not yet failed by time \(\TimeInstant\). If each GPU is independently up with probability \(\GpuAvailability\), all \(\RequiredComponentCount\) are up together with probability \((\GpuAvailability)^\RequiredComponentCount\), which is the reliability gap of equation 1.2. For the chapter’s 10,000-GPU cluster, a per-GPU availability of 99.99 percent (only 52.6 minutes of downtime per year) leaves about a 37 percent chance that every GPU is up at the same instant. That power erodes even highly reliable hardware once \(\RequiredComponentCount\) reaches fleet scale, so software must handle failures automatically.

Systems Perspective 7.1: Scale transforms failure
A cluster of 10,000 GPUs turns a per-GPU MTTF of 50,000 hours into a system MTBF of 5 hours. For a repairable system, steady-state availability is \(\SteadyStateAvailability= \MeanTimeBetweenFailures/(\MeanTimeBetweenFailures+\MeanTimeToRepair)\), where mean time to recovery (MTTR) is the recovery interval. Cutting MTTR from 30 minutes to 5 minutes raises availability from 90.91 percent to 98.36 percent. This stationary two-state model assumes unconstrained repair capacity and complete outage; correlated incidents, repair contention, and degraded operation require direct measurement (Birolini 2017). Systems must engineer recovery, not merely improve components.

Engineering faster recovery is one response to that arithmetic; redundancy is the other. For parallel systems providing redundancy, suppose that any surviving component can satisfy the required service. With independent failures and no repair during the observation interval, the system survives until every component has failed: \[\SystemReliability(\TimeInstant) = 1 - \prod_{i=1}^{\RedundantComponentCount} (1 - {\ComponentReliability}_i(\TimeInstant))\]

Series composition is the training job’s problem, because a synchronous step needs every participant. Parallel composition is the serving fleet’s lever, and it returns when interchangeable replicas protect a latency contract. Both rules, however, describe whatever the model calls a component, and the fault rate assigned to that component depends on which protections sit inside it.

Lighthouse 7.1: Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3): Memory soft-error scale
Composition makes the distinction between raw faults and job interruptions essential. Memory soft-error rates are quoted per megabit of storage rather than per device, so the exposure grows with memory capacity. As an illustrative raw-bit-error budget, assume 250 FIT per megabit across 1,024 GPUs with 80 GB each, the size of the Archetype A reference cluster. The resulting interval between raw memory errors is roughly 20 seconds. This is not a measured job-failure interval. Error-correcting code (ECC) protection can correct covered error patterns before they affect computation, while detected uncorrectable errors and escaped corruption require different recovery paths. At that raw rate, unprotected memory in the reference cluster would corrupt training state many times per hour, so ECC is a precondition for large-scale training rather than an option. A fleet reliability budget therefore needs the residual job-interrupting and silent-corruption rates under its actual protection scheme; raw bit-error rates alone cannot establish whether memory, logic, or interconnect dominates.

These raw bit-error rates justify ECC, but they are not the interruption rates that set checkpoint cadence; the worked cascade that follows uses device MTTF values that already include the effect of that protection. The cascade also treats its GPU, PCIe, power, and network contributions as separate categories, so a measured whole-node rate that already includes those paths cannot be added to them again.

7.2.2 Cluster MTBF calculation

Consider a training cluster designed for large language model development, roughly ten times the 1,024-GPU Archetype A reference cluster, with the following specifications:

  • 10,000 NVIDIA H100 GPUs
  • Individual GPU MTTF: 50,000 hours
  • Each GPU connected to host via PCIe (MTTF: 200,000 hours)
  • Each node contains 8 GPUs fed by 6 power supply units (MTTF: 100,000 hours each) in 4+2 redundancy, so the node keeps full performance with any 2 of them failed
  • Network infrastructure per node (NIC, cables): MTTF 150,000 hours

Each GPU operates within a failure domain that includes the GPU itself, its PCIe connection, and a proportional share of the node’s shared power and network infrastructure. Dividing a node-wide subsystem rate by the 8 GPUs in the node prorates one shared failure source across those GPUs, so that multiplying the per-GPU total by all 10,000 GPUs in the final step counts each node’s shared hardware once per node rather than eight times. Power enters the sum only through supplies whose single failure stops the node, counted as \(\SeriesPsuCount\) in the power equation. With the node’s supplies in redundant sets, no single unit qualifies, so the power term below is zero in this first-order calculation. The approximation omits the residual risk that additional independent failures exhaust redundancy before repair. A fault upstream of the chassis, such as the loss of the circuits feeding it, can also take down the whole node. That risk belongs to the correlated failures treated later in this chapter. The four subsystem rates follow from their MTTF values:

\[ \GpuFailureRate = \frac{1}{50{,}000} = 2.0 \times 10^{-5} \text{ failures/hour} \]

\[ \PcieLinkFailureRate = \frac{1}{200{,}000} = 0.5 \times 10^{-5} \text{ failures/hour} \]

\[ \PerGpuPowerFailureRate = \frac{\SeriesPsuCount}{8} \times \frac{1}{100{,}000} = \frac{0}{8} \times \frac{1}{100{,}000} = 0 \text{ failures/hour} \]

\[ \PerGpuNetworkFailureRate = \frac{1}{8} \times \frac{1}{150{,}000} = 0.083 \times 10^{-5} \text{ failures/hour} \]

Because the subsystems sit in series, their rates add to a per-GPU total:

\[ \PerGpuTotalFailureRate = (2.0 + 0.5 + 0.083) \times 10^{-5} = 2.583 \times 10^{-5} \text{ failures/hour} \]

Multiplying that total by the cluster size gives the system failure rate, whose reciprocal is the cluster MTBF:

\[ \SystemFailureRate = 10{,}000 \times 2.583 \times 10^{-5} = 0.2583 \text{ failures/hour} \]

\[ \SystemMtbf = \frac{1}{0.2583} = 3.87 \text{ hours} \]

Under the model, the cluster experiences a failure every 3.9 hours on average. The expected failure cadence is 6.2 failures/day; a training run lasting one week has an expected count of 43 failures. Actual incident times vary, but the expected loss is large enough that recovery must be part of normal execution planning.

↪ The step-by-step composition of per-component failure rates into a fleet-wide system MTBF, reusable for any node design or cluster size, is developed in The MTBF cascade.

The cluster MTBF scaling in table 1 isolates the GPU-only baseline across cluster sizes. The full-system calculation is lower once PCIe and network failure domains are included. The same 10,000-GPU cluster therefore carries three MTBF values in this chapter. Each later calculation names the one it uses: the GPU-only 5 hours of this table, the full-system 3.9 hours of the cascade above, and the two-to-three-hour cadence that somewhat larger production runs report below. Two running scenarios carry the rest of the chapter. The 1,024-GPU Archetype A reference cluster sizes per-node state, restore paths, and the elastic and straggler examples, while the 10,000-GPU cluster of the cascade sets failure cadence and checkpoint intervals. The full-system MTBF is the default input; a calculation that uses the GPU-only value says so, usually because it compares cluster sizes on the common basis of table 1, and its failure count is then a lower bound.

Production measurements confirm the \(1/\AcceleratorCount\) trend of table 1 and show that the GPU-only line is optimistic. Plotted on log-log axes, measured MTTF values and fitted projections from Meta’s clusters follow the slope of the \(50{,}000\,\text{h}/\AcceleratorCount\) line across more than three orders of magnitude in job size but sit below it. The gap arises because a job can be interrupted by far more than its GPUs (figure 1). The Kokolis et al. (2025) study of Meta’s Research SuperCluster reported measured MTTF values for observed RSC job sizes and projected a 1.8-hour MTTF for 16,384-GPU jobs. That projection lands next to the Llama 3 run previewed in The Scale Moment (war story 7.2 follows its recovery). That run recorded 419 unexpected interruptions across a 54-day snapshot on 16,384 H100 GPUs, which gives an observed MTTF of about 3.1 hours. Together, the measured Llama run and Kokolis projection put 16,384-GPU training on a failure cadence of roughly two to three hours, bracketed by the 1.8-hour projection and the 3.1-hour measurement. This evidence turns the idealized \(1/\AcceleratorCount\) relation into an engineering constraint that determines checkpoint frequency, recovery architecture, and infrastructure investment.

7.3 Failure Taxonomy

A network partition that clears in seconds and a permanent GPU failure both interrupt a training step, yet retrying the first succeeds while retrying the second wastes cluster time on dead silicon. Silent memory corruption that produces wrong gradients demands a different defense again from a node crash that stops responding altogether. While the MTBF calculations quantify how often interruptions arrive, which sets checkpoint intervals and spare-pool sizes, choosing the right recovery action requires distinguishing what kind of failure has occurred. In standard reliability terms, a fault is the underlying defect or disturbance, an error is the corrupted internal state it produces, and a failure is the externally visible misbehavior that the training or serving system must handle (Avizienis et al. 2004).

Avizienis, Algirdas, Jean-Claude Laprie, Brian Randell, and Carl Landwehr. 2004. “Basic Concepts and Taxonomy of Dependable and Secure Computing.” IEEE Transactions on Dependable and Secure Computing 1 (1): 11–33. https://doi.org/10.1109/TDSC.2004.2.

No single label captures all of these differences. Reliability decisions require three orthogonal axes (Constantinescu 2008; Lamport et al. 1982): persistence (transient, intermittent, or permanent), manifestation (fail-stop, degraded, or value-corrupting), and failure scope (one device, one node, a rack or power domain, or the whole job). Two manifestations need names because liveness checks cannot see them. A gray failure is a component that stays alive but runs slowly or erratically, so it keeps answering the liveness checks that would flag a crash while every peer that waits on it slows down. Silent data corruption (SDC) goes further, producing wrong values with no error at all. Because the three axes are independent, a single fault sits on all three at once: a transient disturbance can manifest as either a detected crash or silent corruption, while a permanent switch failure can remain confined to one rack or partition an entire job. Each axis governs a different operational choice. Persistence determines whether a fault clears on its own or requires hardware quarantine and replacement (section 7.4.1). Manifestation separates crashed ranks from slow stragglers (section 7.9.5) and from Byzantine value corruption, while scope determines when a single shared hazard can defeat naive redundancy.

Production logs show which of these failures a large run actually meets. During a 54-day period of Llama 3 405B pretraining on 16,384 H100 GPUs, Meta attributed each of 419 unexpected interruptions to a root-cause component (Dubey et al. 2024). Grouped by cause in figure 2, GPU components account for 268 of them, about 64 percent, led by 148 faulty GPUs and 72 HBM memory failures. Software caused 56, of which 54 were bugs and 2 were failed dependencies. Network switches and cables caused 35, unplanned host maintenance 32, and the remaining host hardware, from NICs to power supplies, 21. The last 7 were NCCL watchdog timeouts whose underlying cause was never found. Only 6 interruptions were attributed to silent data corruption, and that count includes only corruption that was caught. Most fault-tolerance effort therefore goes to frequent, detectable accelerator failures, while the rare silent ones demand a different kind of defense.

7.3.1 Transient failures

A transient fault clears without physical repair, but the state it corrupts can persist after the disturbance ends, forcing recovery to retry or validate affected work before relying on that state. In large-scale training systems, temporary disruptions range from network packet loss, where dropped packets are recovered by transport retransmission, to memory bit flips triggered by cosmic-ray-induced single-event upsets.4 Timing disturbances can be equally disruptive without corrupting bits directly. Under temperature spikes, thermal throttling reduces compute throughput without halting execution. Software timeouts fire when transient contention delays a collective beyond its allotted window.

4 Single-event upset (SEU): From particle physics, where “upset” denotes a nondestructive state change. Cosmic rays and alpha particles from chip packaging can flip bits in memory and logic; the observed rate depends on altitude, process technology, packaging materials, shielding, and workload (Ziegler et al. 1996; Baumann 2005). ECC memory corrects single-bit errors but cannot handle every multi-bit upset in the same word, leaving a residual silent-corruption risk that compounds across the terabytes of state in large-scale ML training.

Ziegler, J. F., H. W. Curtis, H. P. Muhlfeld, C. J. Montrose, B. Chin, M. Nicewicz, C. A. Russell, et al. 1996. “IBM Experiments in Soft Fails in Computer Electronics (1978–1994).” IBM Journal of Research and Development 40 (1): 3–18. https://doi.org/10.1147/rd.401.0003.
Baumann, R. 2005. “Soft Errors in Advanced Computer Systems.” IEEE Design and Test of Computers 22 (3): 258–66. https://doi.org/10.1109/mdt.2005.69.

Transient faults are particularly difficult to diagnose in ML training when they leave no explicit error signal. If hardware protection and the computation itself do not mask a memory bit flip during gradient computation, the corrupted value propagates into later updates while training continues. Large-scale SDC studies document such escaped corruption in production processors, and DRAM field studies confirm that memory errors occur frequently enough to require fleet-level monitoring (Dixit et al. 2021; Sridharan et al. 2015; Schroeder et al. 2009). In ML training, that undetected corruption surfaces as degraded gradients, anomalous loss curves, or rollback-worthy checkpoints rather than an explicit crash.

How the system responds to a transient failure therefore depends on whether the fault announces itself. Errors that raise explicit exceptions can be handled through bounded retry logic, whereas silent corruption requires end-to-end validation. Checksums catch altered bytes in storage or transport, cross-rank digests expose disagreement across a supposedly identical reduced buffer, and statistical or replay checks test whether the computation itself remains plausible. Because a rank that computes the wrong local gradient and then checksums those wrong bytes will pass an ordinary transport check, only independent recomputation, invariants, or model-level evidence can expose the error.

7.3.2 Fail-stop failures

In synchronous distributed training, a single participant that goes silent stalls the entire group’s next update. A conventional AllReduce cannot complete until every rank supplies its input, so one dead GPU in a 10,000-GPU job can leave the other 9,999 accelerators waiting at the barrier until recovery begins. Smaller communication groups limit the immediate dependency, although pipeline or data-parallel stages soon propagate the stall across the rest of the job. Such fail-stop failures occur when a component ceases operation outright and stops responding to requests. A GPU hardware failure renders an individual accelerator unresponsive, and a host node crash terminates all training processes on the machine. A network partition severs communication between a healthy node and the cluster, and a storage failure blocks workers from reading or writing checkpoint shards.

A stopped component gives a clearer symptom than one that keeps returning plausible but incorrect values. Yet a stopped rank announces itself only through silence, so detection rests on timeouts that must separate an outright crash from a slow or isolated host, such as a GPU still writing a large checkpoint while the collective waits. Once a timeout flags the missing participant, the recovery system replaces or restarts it and restores the most recent valid checkpoint; section 7.9.1 sets that timeout threshold once the full recovery-time budget is in view.

7.3.3 Byzantine failures

Where a fail-stop component ceases to participate, a value-corrupting component can keep running while returning incorrect results. A GPU may return wrong gradients without raising an error, a link may deliver a corrupted packet whose error pattern the CRC happens not to cover, or a worker may compute different results for identical inputs and state. Byzantine failures allow arbitrary behavior, including inconsistent messages to different participants (Lamport et al. 1982). ML systems can encounter this broader failure model through silent data corruption or adversarial corruption. Numerical instability and determinism violations also require validation when they break the execution contract, but ordinary rounding differences or intentionally stochastic outputs are not themselves evidence of a faulty worker. The recovery concern is a participant whose accepted contribution can corrupt shared state without a visible crash.

Lamport, Leslie, Robert Shostak, and Marshall Pease. 1982. “The Byzantine Generals Problem.” ACM Transactions on Programming Languages and Systems 4 (3): 382–401. https://doi.org/10.1145/357172.357176.

The two models leave different evidence (figure 3). A fail-stop worker such as W2 falls silent, and its missing heartbeats make it suspect. A Byzantine worker such as W6 keeps sending messages on schedule, so it passes every liveness check while the values it sends are wrong.

In an ML fleet, the most common source of value corruption is not an adversary but hardware that changes a value without raising an error, the silent data corruption already met among transient failures. Its physical causes, the training signals that expose it, and the verification and hot-spare defenses that contain it are developed together in section 7.5, after the hardware faults that produce it. Whatever its source, a Byzantine contribution violates a contract that liveness checks never test. Ordinary data-parallel workers process different batches and need not produce identical local gradients; the required contract is that each contribution correctly represents its assigned computation. A single Byzantine worker can violate that contract and corrupt the averaged gradient, potentially causing training to diverge or converge to a poor solution.

Redundant computation supplies one form of evidence against Byzantine failures. Workers repeating a computation from matching inputs, state, and random-number streams can compare results under a stated numerical tolerance. Statistical outlier detection and invariants provide additional evidence without fully repeating the computation. Each method has a coverage limit. Agreement can miss shared faults, while a statistical anomaly can arise from legitimate data variation. Detection overhead must therefore be evaluated together with the faults the method can expose.

Byzantine-resilient distributed training algorithms exist but impose significant overhead. Krum (Blanchard et al. 2017) keeps the single worker gradient whose nearest neighbors lie closest to it and discards the rest, while coordinate-wise trimmed mean (Yin et al. 2018) drops the largest and smallest values in each coordinate before averaging what remains. Both compute aggregates that stay robust to a bounded number of Byzantine workers, but they require more communication and computation than simple averaging. Without such protection, corrupted gradients can push the optimizer toward an unreliable model state while the training job appears healthy. Reliability must therefore protect semantic correctness rather than process liveness alone.5

Yin, Dong, Yudong Chen, Kannan Ramchandran, and Peter Bartlett. 2018. “Byzantine-Robust Distributed Learning: Towards Optimal Statistical Rates.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 5650–59.

5 Byzantine-resilient ML: Named after Lamport’s 1982 “Byzantine Generals Problem,” these algorithms (Krum, trimmed mean, and signSGD, which aggregates only the signs of gradient coordinates by majority vote) tolerate a bounded fraction of corrupted workers, with the exact bound depending on the rule and its assumptions. Krum’s original analysis requires \(2\ByzantineClientCount + 2 < \KrumClientCount\) for \(\ByzantineClientCount\) Byzantine workers among \(\KrumClientCount\), while coordinate-wise median and trimmed mean rest on different robustness assumptions, so the tolerated fraction depends on the aggregation rule, dimensionality, and statistical model. For \(\KrumClientCount\) workers, Krum requires pairwise distance computations over worker gradients, so overhead grows quadratically with worker count and directly competes with the throughput gains of data parallelism (Blanchard et al. 2017).

Blanchard, Peva, El Mahdi El Mhamdi, Rachid Guerraoui, and Julien Stainer. 2017. “Machine Learning with Adversaries: Byzantine Tolerant Gradient Descent.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS), 119–29.

7.3.4 Correlated failures

The reliability calculations in section 7.2 assume independent failures, yet real systems depart sharply from this assumption when common physical or operational dependencies take down multiple components at once. Within a single node, a power fault that exhausts the chassis’s redundant supplies, such as the loss of its feed circuits, removes every GPU in the chassis. Across a cluster, a top-of-rack network switch failure isolates all attached nodes, while a cooling system failure can trigger thermal shutdowns down an entire row of racks. Shared software and operational dependencies widen the blast radius further. A software bug in an updated CUDA driver can crash processes across nodes whenever they exercise the affected code path, and operator error during cluster reconfiguration can render an entire fleet unreachable.

Correlated failures violate the independence assumption underlying equation 3. A model that counts only independent device failures omits shared hazards and their potentially larger blast radius; incident frequency and the number of affected devices must be modeled separately. Correlated failures can also defeat redundancy strategies. Three replicas of a model provide no protection against a shared power failure if all three run on the same power domain.

Defending against correlated failures requires classifying each failure by its scope, the failure domain it takes down, and ensuring that redundancy spans independent domains. Common failure domains in ML infrastructure range from a single GPU to an entire data center region, and each calls for its own mitigation (table 2). These domains nest, so a physical disruption scales upward from one eight-GPU node to a rack and then to a multi-rack power and network envelope (figure 4).

The containment hierarchy in table 2 and figure 4 explains why replica placement must match the fault being tolerated: placing replicas within the same domain provides zero protection against that domain’s failure mode. The highlighted rack in figure 4 contains nodes that share a top-of-rack (ToR) switch, so that switch’s loss can disconnect every attached node from the job. A neighboring rack with a separate switch can remain reachable during that event even if both racks share a power domain, yet that same placement does not protect against loss of their shared power source. Redundancy must therefore cross the relevant failure boundary, not merely occupy different devices.

↩︎ The failure domain and its physical nesting from node to rack to building are established in Node health and reliability.

Checkpoint 7.1: Mapping failure domains

Verify your understanding of how failure domains nest and their operational impact:

War Story 7.1: The supercomputer that swapped in spare cubes (2024)
Context: Google’s Tensor Processing Unit (TPU) v4 machine-learning supercomputer used 4,096 TPU chips connected by a custom 3D torus inter-chip interconnect (ICI) fabric, and Google engineers published their operating experience with automatic fault resiliency and hardware recovery (Zu et al. 2024).

Mechanism: At 4,096-chip scale, machine and link failures could remove participants or paths required by a job’s configured communication topology.

Impact: Losing a required participant can interrupt the synchronous job across the slice. Losing a link instead raises a routing question: whether the remaining fabric can still provide the required communication paths.

Fix: Google used optical circuit switches (OCS) to assemble replacement slices from spare healthy TPU cubes, the pod’s building blocks of chips wired as a \(4{\times}4{\times}4\) mesh, for machine outages, together with fault-tolerant routing for supported OCS outage patterns. The response depended on which resource failed.

Systems lesson: Fault tolerance is a topology-management problem. In tightly coupled accelerator fleets, recovery must preserve the communication graph, not merely replace an individual component; otherwise one local hardware fault can become a cluster-wide training interruption.

Self-Check: Question
  1. Sequence the following failure domains in ascending order of their physical containment hierarchy and typical blast radius (from smallest number of affected GPUs to largest):
  1. Top-of-rack (ToR) switch failure isolating 32–64 GPUs
  2. Single GPU hardware lockup affecting 1 GPU
  3. Multi-rack Power Distribution Unit (PDU) failure affecting 100–500 GPUs
  4. Node-level power loss (a fault that exhausts a node’s redundant PSUs) affecting 8 GPUs
  5. Regional power grid disruption affecting all GPUs in a data center

  1. During a distributed AllReduce operation, a single GPU rank experiences a silent bit flip in an exponent calculation. The rank does not crash, passes network timeouts, and contributes an anomalously large gradient to the collective average. How is this failure categorized, and what is its primary danger to the training run?

    1. Byzantine failure; it silently poisons model weights and gradients while bypassing standard process-liveness and timeout detection.
    2. Fail-stop failure; it immediately halts the AllReduce collective and triggers an automated container restart.
    3. Transient network failure; it is automatically corrected by underlying InfiniBand packet retransmission protocols.
    4. Benign gray failure; it is absorbed by stochastic gradient descent with zero impact on final convergence.

See Answers →

7.4 Hardware Faults at Fleet Scale

A cosmic ray that flips one bit in a GPU’s high-bandwidth memory and a solder joint that cracks under thermal cycling are physical events, but the recovery system never observes them directly. It observes symptoms: a gradient spike with no process crash, a rank that disappears from the collective, or a node that fails only after it heats under sustained load. The failure taxonomy of section 7.3 already classifies such events by persistence, manifestation, and scope. For hardware, persistence carries most of the operational weight because it decides whether the fleet should retry, quarantine, or replace a component, while manifestation decides whether the fleet notices the fault at all.

ML workloads raise the stakes of each fault. A training step performs trillions of arithmetic operations on state that persists into later steps, so a wrong value can survive beyond the computation that produced it. The damage depends on the affected value and its later use. Under the IEEE 754 format,6 a sign-bit flip inverts a weight, while an exponent-bit flip can move a small gradient by many orders of magnitude. Fault-injection studies show that network resilience varies with model, layer, and bit position, so a few faults in vulnerable locations can cost disproportionate accuracy (Reagen et al. 2018). A valid-looking corrupted value can enter learned state without triggering the process failure that would otherwise initiate recovery.

6 IEEE 754: The IEEE 754 floating-point standard defines the binary32 format with 1 sign bit, 8 exponent bits, and 23 fraction bits (IEEE Standards Association 2019). Because bit positions differ so much in consequence, resilience mechanisms often prioritize the sign and exponent bits rather than treating all bits as equally important.

IEEE Standards Association. 2019. IEEE 754-2019: Standard for Floating-Point Arithmetic. https://doi.org/10.1109/IEEESTD.2019.8766229.
Reagen, B., U. Gupta, L. Pentecost, P. Whatmough, S. K. Lee, N. Mulholland, D. Brooks, and G.-Y. Wei. 2018. “Ares: A Framework for Quantifying the Resilience of Deep Neural Networks.” 2018 55th ACM/ESDA/IEEE Design Automation Conference (DAC), 1–6. https://doi.org/10.1109/dac.2018.8465834.

↪ How an FP16 exponent-bit flip rescales a value by \(2^{16}\) and can saturate an attention softmax is developed in Silent Data Corruption in Attention.

Fleet scale turns these rare device events into routine operating conditions. Smaller geometries and lower operating voltages can reduce the charge needed to disturb a stored value (Baumann 2005). Fleet studies at Google and Meta found that a small but persistent population of processors, on the order of a few per several thousand machines, computes wrong results without raising any error (Hochschild et al. 2021; Dixit et al. 2021). Studies of accelerator training fleets reach the same conclusion: rare hardware faults appear often enough that software-level detection and recovery become part of the training system design (He et al. 2023). ML system architects must therefore treat hardware as an unreliable substrate and budget for end-to-end integrity checks, replayed computation on high-value paths, and periodic consistency checks as system requirements rather than high-performance computing specialties.

7.4.1 Fault persistence and the fleet response

Each persistence class from the taxonomy maps to a different fleet action, from retry for a fault that leaves nothing to repair, through quarantine for one that returns under load, to replacement for one that repeats on every attempt (figure 5). A transient fault, as section 7.3.1 established, leaves no lasting defect from that event, so the fleet’s question is only whether its effect escaped. A single-event upset, a voltage droop on an unstable power path (Reddi and Gupta 2013), a timing violation, or a soft error in combinational logic (Mukherjee et al. 2005) can corrupt a tensor, packet, or instruction. Detection and correction can prevent propagation; affected state may otherwise require rollback. During training, an undetected error in weights or gradients can compromise convergence and contaminate subsequent checkpoints (He et al. 2023). During stateless inference, an activation error can remain confined to one request (Mahmoud et al. 2020). If that activation enters retained state such as a KV cache, later outputs can also depend on it.

Reddi, Vijay Janapa, and Meeta Sharma Gupta. 2013. Resilient Architecture Design for Voltage Variation. Synthesis Lectures on Computer Architecture. Springer International Publishing. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-01739-1.
Mukherjee, S. S., J. Emer, and S. K. Reinhardt. 2005. “The Soft Error Problem: An Architectural Perspective.” 11th International Symposium on High-Performance Computer Architecture, 243–47. https://doi.org/10.1109/hpca.2005.37.

A permanent fault announces itself through repeatability. The same accelerator, memory cell, or link fails again after retry, often at the same address or in the same workload phase. Manufacturing defects and wear-out mechanisms such as electromigration7 and oxide breakdown produce this signature, and the stuck-at fault, in which a signal remains fixed at 0 or 1 regardless of input, is its standard abstract model (Seong et al. 2010). Repeatability helps locate the defect but does not make the result trustworthy. A permanent datapath fault keeps producing wrong gradients until the device is drained from the cluster (He et al. 2023), and rerunning the computation on the same device reproduces the error instead of exposing it. The fleet must stop trusting the component, quarantine it, and restore from a checkpoint that predates its first bad write.

7 Electromigration: Gradual displacement of metal atoms in conductors by electron momentum transfer, first characterized in early electromigration studies and later modeled in Black’s reliability equation (Black 1969). Mean time to failure decreases with higher current density and higher temperature, so sustained high-power accelerator workloads make thermal management a direct determinant of fleet lifespan.

Black, James R. 1969. “Electromigration–a Brief Survey and Some Recent Results.” IEEE Transactions on Electron Devices 16 (4): 338–47. https://doi.org/10.1109/T-ED.1969.16754.
Seong, N. H., D. H. Woo, V. Srinivasan, J. A. Rivers, and H.-H. S. Lee. 2010. “SAFER: Stuck-at-Fault Error Recovery for Memories.” 2010 43rd Annual IEEE/ACM International Symposium on Microarchitecture, 115–24. https://doi.org/10.1109/micro.2010.46.
He, Yi, Mike Hutton, Steven Chan, Robert De Gruijl, Rama Govindaraju, Nishant Patil, and Yanjing Li. 2023. “Understanding and Mitigating Hardware Failures in Deep Learning Training Systems.” Proceedings of the 50th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture, 1–16. https://doi.org/10.1145/3579371.3589105.
Rashid, Layali, Karthik Pattabiraman, and Sathish Gopalakrishnan. 2015. “Characterizing the Impact of Intermittent Hardware Faults on Programs.” IEEE Transactions on Reliability 64 (1): 297–310. https://doi.org/10.1109/tr.2014.2363152.
Zhang, Jeff, Kartheek Rangineni, Zahra Ghodsi, and Siddharth Garg. 2018. “ThUnderVolt: Enabling Aggressive Voltage Underscaling and Timing Error Resilience for Energy Efficient Deep Learning Accelerators.” 2018 55th ACM/ESDA/IEEE Design Automation Conference (DAC), 1–6. https://doi.org/10.1109/dac.2018.8465918.

Intermittent faults create the most operational ambiguity because they produce evidence and then disappear. A node may pass a reboot test, rejoin the fleet, and fail again only when the next job drives the package into the same thermal, voltage, or communication regime. Cracked solder joints, aging packages, and contamination between contacts create these load-dependent conditions (Constantinescu 2008; Rashid et al. 2015), and reduced voltage margins cause timing errors that are hard to reproduce (Zhang et al. 2018). Sporadic errors of this kind can accumulate across iterations and degrade convergence without any explicit failure (Rashid et al. 2015). An intermittent node should therefore remain suspect until evidence clears it. The fleet drains it from long jobs, correlates its error logs, and readmits it only after it passes stress tests under the load that exposed the fault. Otherwise one marginal component can make validation and recovery untrustworthy for every job that lands on it. Across the three persistence classes, the evidence that exposes a fault also determines the fleet’s response (table 3).

7.4.2 Hardware lifecycle and the hazard rate

A newly unboxed accelerator rack is far more likely to fail in its first week of service than in its sixth month, and a multi-year-old rack that has endured thousands of thermal cycles becomes fragile again as packages and solder joints fatigue. While persistence classifies a fault after it strikes, the hazard function \(\ComponentHazardRate(\TimeInstant)\) describes when over a component’s operating lifespan faults are most likely to occur, partitioning hardware longevity into three reliability regimes (figure 6). The first phase, infant mortality, brings elevated failure rates from manufacturing defects, improper installation, and early-life failure of marginal components. For electronic components it typically lasts days to weeks, which is why burn-in testing8 stresses hardware before deployment to precipitate those early failures prior to production use.

8 Burn-in testing: A controlled screening procedure stresses components before deployment to expose early-life defects. Temperature, voltage, duration, and acceptance criteria depend on the component and its qualified test procedure; a generic semiconductor screening recipe is not an operating specification for an assembled accelerator. Screening reduces the defective population entering service but does not certify every later workload or fault mode.

Birolini, A. 2017. Reliability Engineering: Theory and Practice. 8th ed. Springer Berlin Heidelberg. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-662-54209-5.
Klutke, G., P. C. Kiessler, and M. A. Wortman. 2003. “A Critical Look at the Bathtub Curve.” IEEE Transactions on Reliability 52 (1): 125–29. https://doi.org/10.1109/tr.2002.804492.

After surviving infant mortality, components enter the useful-life phase, where failure rates remain relatively constant under the standard reliability-engineering model (Birolini 2017; Klutke et al. 2003). This phase spans the longest portion of a component’s lifetime and is the regime where the exponential reliability model in equation 1 applies most accurately. For data center GPUs, the useful-life window is an operational planning concept shaped by refresh cycles, cooling, utilization, and observed fleet health rather than a fixed physical duration.

As components age, they enter the wear-out phase, where failure rates increase due to accumulated wear. For GPUs, wear mechanisms include electromigration in circuits, thermal cycling stress on solder joints, and degradation of thermal interface materials. The onset of wear-out depends heavily on operating conditions; components operated at high temperatures or with frequent thermal cycling enter wear-out earlier.

The practical implication for ML systems is that fleet-wide failure rates can depend on age distribution. Under the bathtub model, a new cohort passes through elevated early-life risk, a relatively stable period, and eventual wear-out. Screening, operating conditions, and replacement policy determine how much of that pattern appears in field data. Mixed-age fleets combine cohorts at different stages, so their aggregate hazard need not follow the curve of a single cohort.

These three hazard regimes in figure 6 translate directly into admission control and maintenance policies. Before assigning new or repaired nodes to a long training run, operators use matrix-multiply, memory, and communication stress tests to expose marginal components. During service, temperature trends, error counts, and performance degradation help identify devices for scheduled replacement. Screening moves failures out of expensive production runs. An infant-mortality failure caught during a few hours of stress testing costs only idle time, while the same failure weeks into a pretraining run forces a checkpoint rollback. For that reason operators often route fresh or repaired nodes to short jobs first. Monitoring then checks whether the constant-rate model remains appropriate as the fleet ages.

7.4.3 Fleet-scale detection and mitigation

Burn-in screening and lifecycle replacement keep the most failure-prone hardware out of long training runs, but even healthy mid-life devices still suffer random faults that compound across a fleet just as the interruption rates of section 7.2 do. Field studies of DRAM in large data centers found memory errors frequent enough that error-correcting memory, background scrubbing, and error-rate monitoring are standard fleet practice rather than optional hardening (Schroeder et al. 2009; Sridharan et al. 2015). Protection consumes some capacity and bandwidth for check bits and scrubbing, but that cost is small compared with a corrupted multi-week run.

Schroeder, Bianca, Eduardo Pinheiro, and Wolf-Dietrich Weber. 2009. “DRAM Errors in the Wild: A Large-Scale Field Study.” ACM SIGMETRICS Performance Evaluation Review 37 (1): 193–204. https://doi.org/10.1145/2492101.1555372.

9 Hamming codes (1950): Richard Hamming developed error-correcting codes at Bell Labs after relay computers repeatedly detected errors in his unattended weekend jobs and abandoned them rather than repairing them. A Hamming code uses parity checks to locate a single-bit error with \(\mathcal{O}(\log \HammingDataBitCount)\) check-bit overhead; adding an overall parity bit yields the extended single-error-correcting, double-error-detecting form. Memory ECC implementations vary in code and coverage, so the system must account for both corrected errors and residual faults outside that coverage.

Hamming, R. W. 1950. “Error Detecting and Error Correcting Codes.” Bell System Technical Journal 29 (2): 147–60. https://doi.org/10.1002/j.1538-7305.1950.tb00463.x.

10 CRC (cyclic redundancy check): Polynomial checksum family introduced by Peterson and Brown (1961). CRC coverage depends on the polynomial, frame length, and error model; it is best understood as a low-cost detection layer rather than a universal guarantee. In distributed ML training, checksums or hashes can validate gradient payloads exchanged during collectives; without a verification layer, a corrupted gradient packet can silently poison the parameter update for every worker in the collective.

Peterson, W. W., and D. T. Brown. 1961. “Cyclic Codes for Error Detection.” Proceedings of the IRE 49 (1): 228–35. https://doi.org/10.1109/jrproc.1961.287814.
Bushnell, Michael L, and Vishwani D Agrawal. 2002. “Built-in Self-Test.” Essentials of Electronic Testing for Digital, Memory and Mixed-Signal VLSI Circuits 1: 489–548. https://doi.org/10.1007/0-306-47040-3_15.
Hochschild, Peter H., Paul Turner, Jeffrey C. Mogul, Rama Govindaraju, Parthasarathy Ranganathan, David E. Culler, and Amin Vahdat. 2021. “Cores That Don’t Count.” Proceedings of the Workshop on Hot Topics in Operating Systems, 9–16. https://doi.org/10.1145/3458336.3465297.

Detection works in layers with different coverage and cost. At the hardware layer, error-correcting codes9 (Hamming 1950) correct and detect the memory-error patterns covered by the implemented code, and link-level CRCs10 check corruption in transit. These protections operate within their enabled storage and transport boundaries; they do not validate an incorrect arithmetic result merely because its bytes arrive intact. Before deployment, built-in self-test and the burn-in screening of the hardware lifecycle expose manufacturing defects and permanent faults (Bushnell and Agrawal 2002). During service, operators periodically pull nodes from production and run known-answer stress tests on arithmetic, memory, and interconnect, because defective processors often fail only on particular instruction mixes, data patterns, or operating points (Hochschild et al. 2021; Dixit et al. 2021).

The software layer draws its cheapest evidence from metrics the training loop already emits. Statistical checks on loss, gradient norms, and activations flag silent drift (Chandola et al. 2009), missed heartbeats identify suspected failures (Kawazoe Aguilera et al. 1997), and watchdog timers catch tasks that stall without crashing (Pont and Ong 2002). A node that stays alive but runs slowly or erratically, the gray failure of the failure taxonomy, can degrade progress while still meeting a liveness timeout, so section 7.9.5 treats it through straggler detection. Redundant computation adds stronger evidence on selected paths, either periodically or in response to an anomaly, by replaying a step on another device or comparing independently computed results. Its frequency depends on the residual corruption risk, the value of the protected state, and the cost of detecting an error late.

Chandola, Varun, Arindam Banerjee, and Vipin Kumar. 2009. “Anomaly Detection: A Survey.” ACM Computing Surveys 41 (3): 1–58. https://doi.org/10.1145/1541880.1541882.
Kawazoe Aguilera, Marcos, Wei Chen, and Sam Toueg. 1997. “Heartbeat: A Timeout-Free Failure Detector for Quiescent Reliable Communication.” In Lect. Notes Comput. Sci. Springer Berlin Heidelberg. https://doi.org/10.1007/bfb0030680.
Pont, Michael J, and Royan HL Ong. 2002. “Using Watchdog Timers to Improve the Reliability of Single-Processor Embedded Systems: Seven New Patterns and a Case Study.” Proceedings of the First Nordic Conference on Pattern Languages of Programs, 159–200.
Checkpoint 7.2: Classifying a hardware fault

Verify your understanding of the transient, permanent, and intermittent fault categories and the fault, error, failure progression:

Self-Check: Question

  1. A cluster node passes cold-boot diagnostics and memory tests during morning maintenance, but repeatedly produces incorrect matrix multiplication results under sustained thermal load during large-batch backward passes. What type of fault does this represent, and what is the appropriate operational response?

    1. Transient fault; retry the exact batch on the same node since the fault will disappear automatically.
    2. Intermittent fault; collect runtime telemetry under load, quarantine the node, and replace or repair it rather than returning it directly to production.
    3. Permanent stuck-at-0 fault; immediately discard all training checkpoints because the device cannot execute any instructions.
    4. Software race condition; upgrade the host OS kernel and increase the AllReduce heartbeat timeout threshold.

  2. Compare the impact of a single-bit flip in the sign bit versus a single-bit flip in the exponent bits of an IEEE 754 floating-point weight or gradient tensor during neural network training.

  3. A node that stays alive and keeps answering heartbeats but runs slowly or erratically, so that timeouts never fire, is said to suffer a ____ failure.

  4. How does a permanent stuck-at fault in a specialized accelerator’s Tensor Core multiply-accumulate unit manifest differently during training compared to a traditional processor crash?

    1. It causes an immediate kernel execution timeout that terminates the local process group within milliseconds.
    2. It corrupts operating system page tables, forcing an immediate kernel panic and node reboot.
    3. It triggers a PCIe bus reset that automatically redirects subsequent GEMM operations to host CPU memory.
    4. It can produce repeatable incorrect values in computations using the defective datapath while the process remains alive.

  5. Because electronic components exhibit a constant failure rate during their useful life phase under the exponential reliability model, cluster operators should assign newly delivered accelerator nodes directly to multi-week foundation model pretraining runs without prior burn-in testing.

7.5 Check-and-Verify Defenses Against Silent Data Corruption

Of the hardware faults just classified, the costliest are the ones whose manifestation is silent. A corrupted value that raises no error passes every liveness check the fleet runs, and training keeps building on it. Defending against silent data corruption therefore requires checks that establish a correct result, not merely a live process, and choosing those checks starts with where the corruption comes from and how it surfaces in training.

7.5.1 The physics of silent corruption

An accelerator executes deterministic logic gates, yet the silicon carrying those gates operates under narrow physical margins where both random particle strikes and load-dependent defects can flip bits without halting the processor. Single-event upsets (SEUs), the particle strikes introduced with transient failures, create fleet exposure that grows with device count and operating time even when the per-device rate is low. Manufacturing variances can instead leave “marginal” chips that pass initial QA but fail under particular inputs or operating conditions. Voltage and temperature margins matter, including supply disturbances during rapid current changes (\(d\AcceleratorSupplyCurrent/d\TimeInstant\)). Because these margin defects produce repeatable errors under specific workloads while particle strikes arrive at random, fleet reliability requires a probabilistic exposure model even when individual hardware faults are deterministic.

Facebook documented a pervasive SDC issue where a hardware fault caused a valid file to be reported as “size zero” during decompression (Dixit et al. 2021); the system “worked” without crashing, but data was silently deleted. In ML, the same failure manifests as valid-looking but numerically corrupted gradients. The pipeline trace in figure 7 follows a single ALU or memory bit flip as it corrupts an individual tensor entry, taints gradient reduction during the backward pass, commits into weights, and finally surfaces as degraded quality at evaluation. The comparative timelines beneath contrast this silent latency with a standard detected error. A hardware trap halts within milliseconds to restart cleanly from the last checkpoint, whereas silent corruption commits contaminated state into checkpoints over hours or days before an evaluation alert forces a costly rollback.

Telemetry from production training fleets shows this corruption is routine rather than anomalous. At modern training scales, hardware bit flips occur regularly, and erroneous computations from a single malfunctioning accelerator can propagate into the global model state, as Jeff Dean documented in his MLSys 2024 keynote on silent data corruption (Dean 2024) (figure 8).

In the Google training telemetry presented by Dean (2024), figure 9 identifies an abrupt gradient-norm anomaly as an SDC event. The plot shows the symptom without identifying the damaged bit or circuit. Corrupting a floating-point exponent is one mechanism that can greatly change a tensor value and subsequent updates. Gradient clipping can limit update magnitude, but it does not reconstruct the correct gradient; an anomaly detector must trigger validation or recovery before suspect state is accepted.

7.5.2 Verification at fleet scale

Cumulative SDC probability nears certainty over a long run.

Even a small per-GPU SDC rate accumulates across repeated collective operations, and the accumulation grows with fleet size. Any single collective is unlikely to contain an SDC event. Under the illustrative rate used in the analysis that follows, the per-step probability remains small while the cumulative probability grows over a long training run, reaching \(1 - e^{-1} \approx 63\) percent once the expected number of events reaches one. On the chapter’s 10,000-GPU cluster, that rate gives one expected event roughly every 100 hours, or about 22 over the three-month run of the chapter opening. The notebook below scales the same estimate to a fleet ten times larger to show where the trend leads, since the expected interval shrinks in proportion to fleet size. Standard AllReduce reduces the supplied contributions; it does not establish that an alive worker computed a correct gradient. The fleet therefore needs validation appropriate to its fault model in addition to liveness checks.

Check-and-verify methods test data-path invariants, subject to the boundary drawn for transient failures in section 7.3.1. Digests test byte integrity and agreement within their error-detection limits; they cannot establish that the original computation was correct. Independent computation, an algorithmic invariant, or replay tests that additional claim under a declared fault model. A quick estimate shows how much exposure those checks must cover.

Napkin Math 7.1: SDC risk accumulation
Problem: Under an illustrative generic SDC-rate assumption, calculate the probability that at least one GPU in a 100,000-GPU fleet experiences an SDC event during a single 2 s training step.

  1. Fleet size: 100,000 accelerators.
  2. Individual risk: \(10^{-6}\) per GPU-hour (an illustrative generic SDC-rate assumption).
  3. The exposure: In a 2-second window, the fleet has \(100{,}000 \times (2/3600) \approx 56\) “GPU-hours” of exposure.
  4. The probability: \(\Pr(\text{at least one SDC}) \approx\) 0.0056 percent.

Systems insight: Under this illustrative constant-rate assumption, a 100k-GPU fleet expects one SDC event every 18,000 steps (roughly every 10 hours), even though the per-step probability is only 0.0056 percent. A CRC or hash can protect each shard or reduced buffer against corruption in transit or storage, while a redundant reduction can test the arithmetic path independently. Robustness moves from being a restart problem to a verification problem only when the verification covers the failure mechanism being claimed.

Full replication protects a broader computation path at a correspondingly high resource cost. Dual modular redundancy runs every computation twice and compares the results, and triple modular redundancy runs it three times and takes a majority vote. These schemes pay 100 and 200 percent hardware overhead, respectively, to detect or mask a fault within a single control deadline (Sheaffer et al. 2007; Arifeen et al. 2020). These guarantees depend on the replicas avoiding a shared fault. The price is justified in safety-critical embedded control, such as a vehicle’s onboard computers, where one wrong output can endanger people and the replicated hardware is a small computer. For fleet training, duplicating every step of a run on 10,000 GPUs doubles the compute devoted to those steps, before comparison and coordination costs. When corruption is rare and rollback can recover valid state, selective redundancy can protect useful progress at lower cost. Screening removes suspect devices, statistical checks run continuously, standby pools absorb drained nodes, and periodic or triggered recomputation checks selected execution paths.

Sheaffer, J. W., D. P. Luebke, and K. Skadron. 2007. “A Hardware Redundancy and Recovery Mechanism for Reliable Scientific Computation on Graphics Processors.” Graphics Hardware 2007: 55–64. https://doi.org/10.2312/eggh/eggh07/055-064.
Arifeen, Tooba, Abdus Sami Hassan, and Jeong-A Lee. 2020. “Approximate Triple Modular Redundancy: A Survey.” IEEE Access 8: 139851–67. https://doi.org/10.1109/access.2020.3012673.

Google’s reactive hot-spare protocol shows how those pieces combine in three stages (figure 10). When runtime monitors flag an anomalous gradient and replay implicates a suspect accelerator (G2), the scheduler isolates that device and promotes a standby hot spare (S) into the active ring. It then provisions a replacement spare (S’) while the suspect unit undergoes hardware diagnostics (Dean 2024). The fleet pays in advance for one idle device per pool rather than for a duplicate of every computation, and the protocol shifts the burden of correctness from unverified hardware to system-level auditing. Even so, these hardware checks reduce only the risk of an unreliable physical substrate; they do not protect against faults that the software stack introduces itself, bugs that look like valid computation while corrupting the ML pipeline.

Checkpoint 7.3: Detecting silent corruption

Verify your understanding of Byzantine failures and SDC:

Self-Check: Question

  1. In a 100,000-GPU training cluster where each device has an illustrative SDC probability of \(10^{-6}\) per GPU-hour, what is the expected SDC exposure and failure cadence during continuous AllReduce training with 2-second step times?

    1. The cluster experiences roughly 55.6 GPU-hours of exposure per 2-second step, resulting in an expected SDC event approximately every 18,000 steps (every ~10 hours).
    2. The cluster experiences an SDC event on every single step because 100,000 GPUs exceed the reliability threshold.
    3. The cluster experiences an SDC event once every 1,000 days because \(10^{-6}\) is negligible for 2-second steps.
    4. The cluster experiences an SDC event exactly every 50,000 hours regardless of cluster size.

  2. Explain what gradient checksums and redundant computation can each detect during AllReduce, and why checksums alone cannot establish the arithmetic correctness of every worker contribution.

  3. Safety-critical embedded systems use dual or triple modular redundancy to detect or mask hardware faults. Explain why the same approach does not transfer to a 10,000-GPU training fleet, and describe how fleets spend redundancy instead.

  4. A component that remains active but sends arbitrary, corrupted, or conflicting data requires ____ fault tolerance reasoning rather than only fail-stop detection.

7.6 Software Faults

A team spends three months testing a large language model (LLM) against complex prompt-level failure cases, only to realize that its preprocessing script kept only the last 512 tokens of each input, silently discarding the system prompt on long requests. The example exposes an ordinary source of ML failure that algorithmic testing can miss. A logic error in a data loader or prompt assembler can preserve valid tensor shapes while changing the computation’s meaning, allowing the pipeline to continue without a crash.

Software faults require a different recovery lens from hardware failures. Hardware faults enter from silicon, electrons, and physical wear; software faults enter through design choices, dependency versions, and pipeline glue. The fault-tolerance problem shifts from replacing a failed component to identifying which valid-looking transformation corrupted data, gradients, or predictions. Every boundary between framework, library, runtime, and distributed system is a place where such a transformation can hide.

7.6.1 Software fault properties and propagation

A single tensor reference leaked on every training iteration lets a correctly sized job drift toward the device memory limit over hundreds of steps and then crash with an out-of-memory error far from the code that leaked it. Across ML frameworks, software faults of this kind span logic errors, resource leaks, concurrency bugs, and integration mismatches that propagate across system boundaries. Race conditions in asynchronous updates can lose or overwrite parameter updates, and deadlocks11 leave workers waiting on messages that never arrive. Because many of these faults surface only under high load, on particular hardware, or with a specific pairing of a high-level framework and a low-level numerical library such as cuDNN, root-cause analysis must treat the model code, the runtime, and its dependencies as one versioned artifact.

11 Pipeline deadlock: In pipelined training, a deadlock can form when stage 1 waits for a free buffer to send activations to stage 2 while stage 2 waits for stage 1 to accept its backward gradients. Neither stage can proceed, and because every other stage depends on them, the whole job stalls with no error raised, which a collective timeout eventually converts into a detectable failure.

7.6.2 Software fault detection and prevention

The prompt-truncation bug from the opening example and the memory or synchronization bugs above should never survive unchallenged into a multi-week run. Because many software faults leave the pipeline running while corrupting inputs or gradients, each verification gate must establish which invariant holds and which recovery action is safe when it fails. A unit test catches the tokenizer boundary before launch, an integration replay verifies that a recorded batch reproduces the expected shapes and state on known-good hardware, and a fault-injection test verifies that a corrupted buffer or lost worker triggers rollback before tainting committed state.

Several of these gates also test the recovery machinery itself before an incident relies on it. A restore drill verifies a checkpoint manifest, the record that names every saved shard and the training step they share (section 7.8.5.2). A membership fault-injection test kills or partitions a worker and checks that, once replaced, the stale process can no longer write state the job will trust under the epoch, lease, and fencing rules of section 7.9.2. Every recovery drill is judged against two bounds formalized in section 7.8: how much completed work a failure may lose and how long recovery may take before useful execution resumes. Across all six gates, each test earns its place through the operational decision it authorizes (table 4).

These safeguards matter only when each gate targets a concrete corruption path. A test that checks only whether the tokenizer runs is too weak; it must assert that system prompts, labels, masks, and sequence-length metadata survive preprocessing with the intended semantics. Integration replay then follows one recorded batch through loading, sharding, collation, and model input construction. Fault injection tests whether the detector fires before the protected commit and whether recovery stays within the named bounds on lost work and recovery time for the injected fault. Restore drills, stale-writer rejection, and corrupted-shard rejection therefore provide evidence that generic build success cannot supply, and section 7.14 exercises those claims directly once the recovery paths themselves have been built. Before building those paths, however, a fleet must decide how much protection each workload is worth.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why are GPU memory leaks in 70B+ parameter distributed training pipelines particularly destructive and difficult to diagnose compared to standard CPU memory leaks?

    1. GPU memory is dynamically swapped to NVMe disk automatically, causing unpredictable I/O thrashing rather than out-of-memory errors.
    2. GPU allocators immediately throw segmentation faults upon the first unreleased tensor, preventing any batch execution.
    3. Accelerator memory is scarce (40–80 GB per device) and shared across weights, optimizer state, and activations; a small leak per batch gradually builds over hundreds of steps until triggering an un-checkpointed OOM crash mid-run.
    4. GPU memory leaks only occur when using mixed-precision BF16 formats and disappear entirely when switching to FP32 weights.

  2. Contrast a pipeline deadlock with a data race condition in distributed parallel training in terms of their system symptoms and impact on model convergence.

  3. Justify why a silent data preprocessing defect (such as unintended prompt truncation or incorrect token masking) presents a much higher compute and financial risk than a syntax error that crashes on the first batch.

  4. A preprocessing change silently truncates prompts and discards the system prompt. Sequence the reliability gates in the order that should catch the defect, from earliest and cheapest to the final proof that recovery works:

  1. A fault-injection test showing that the resulting alert blocks publication or rolls back to a known-good state
  2. A boundary unit test asserting that tokenizer limits, system prompts, and masks survive preprocessing
  3. An integration replay that follows one recorded batch through loading, sharding, collation, and model input construction

See Answers →

7.7 The Cost of Failure

Losing an uncheckpointed day on a 10,000-GPU foundation-model run burns hundreds of thousands of dollars in accelerator time, whereas losing an hour on a small vision job costs a few hundred dollars and a failed update in an online recommender costs live revenue rather than batch rework.
