Hardware, silent corruption, and software together set how often a fleet fails, but the work a failure destroys determines what each incident costs. A defense is worth buying only where it removes more of that expected loss than it adds in standing overhead.

7.7.1 Failure cost by workload

Three workload properties set the price of a training interruption: the burn rate of the cluster running the job, the footprint of state that must be saved and reloaded, and how sensitive model quality is to lost or reordered updates. Those three factors separate multi-terabyte foundation models and continuous recommenders from smaller vision, speech, and scientific workloads (table 5).

Large language model training illustrates how a costly allocation magnifies each hour of lost progress. The chapter’s 10,000-GPU cluster at approximately $2/GPU-hour incurs $0.5M in compute costs per day. A job checkpointed only once a day can lose up to a full day of progress to one failure, which costs $0.5M in wasted compute plus schedule delay. At the checkpoint interval derived in section 7.8.1, the same failure costs minutes of rework instead of a day. The checkpoint overhead is correspondingly large. At the 14-byte-per-parameter resumable format that the checkpointing analysis uses (BF16 weights, FP32 master weights, and two FP32 Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam) moments), the 175B-parameter running example writes 2.45 TB at every save.

7.7.2 Investing in protection

Every GPU cycle, network byte, and storage drive dedicated to checkpointing, standby nodes, or verification is a resource that is not training the model, yet skimping on protection leaves a multi-million-dollar run exposed to day-long rollbacks. Rational investment in fault tolerance therefore weighs what a failure destroys against what the defense costs to run.

When a failure strikes, its bill extends well beyond the GPU-hours burned on steps that must be replayed. Repeating lost work delays the delivery of the trained model, ties up accelerators that could have run other jobs, and pulls engineers away from research to diagnose faults and nurse manual restarts. On the other side of the cost account, prevention carries a continuous overhead even when the cluster is healthy. Checkpoint storage scales with model size and save frequency, and checkpoint writes compete for memory and network bandwidth. Hot spares and automated recovery controllers consume standing infrastructure, and fault-tolerant coordination adds software complexity that must be maintained and debugged.

Optimal investment balances these two curves against cluster scale. For a small job on a few GPUs where hardware failures are weeks apart, infrequent checkpoints and manual restarts are cheaper than standing recovery infrastructure. On thousands of GPUs where failures arrive several times a day, frequent checkpoints, automated failover, and elastic resizing pay back their overhead many times over. The checkpoint interval derived in section 7.8.1 applies this exact marginal balance to a single decision variable, choosing the cadence where the cost of one more save equals the expected rework it prevents.

Equation 5 presents a simplified economic model for expected cost per training run:

\[ \TotalTrainingRunCost= \BaseTrainingComputeCost+ \mathbb{E}[\TrainingFailureCount] \times \FailureEventCost+ \FaultToleranceCost \tag{5}\]

where \(\BaseTrainingComputeCost\) is the base compute cost, \(\mathbb{E}[\TrainingFailureCount]\) is the expected number of failures during training, \(\FailureEventCost\) is the cost per failure, and \(\FaultToleranceCost\) is the cost of fault tolerance mechanisms. The cost per failure includes wasted compute plus overhead.

Equation 6 formalizes when fault tolerance investment is justified:

\[ \frac{\partial \FaultToleranceCost}{\partial \FaultToleranceInvestment} < -\frac{\partial (\mathbb{E}[\TrainingFailureCount] \times \FailureEventCost)}{\partial \FaultToleranceInvestment} \tag{6}\]

where \(\FaultToleranceInvestment\) represents investment in fault tolerance mechanisms and the base compute cost is held fixed. The negative derivative on the right is the marginal reduction in expected failure cost. Additional protection reduces total expected cost while this reduction exceeds its marginal cost; an interior optimum equates the two when the cost functions have a suitable minimum.

One investment worked through the criterion shows what it decides. Let \(\FaultToleranceInvestment\) be a hot-spare node held idle beside the chapter’s 10,000-GPU cluster, so that a failed node’s replacement no longer waits for the scheduler to find capacity. Its marginal cost is 8 GPUs idling all day, about $384 per day. Its marginal benefit is the recovery time it removes from each failure while every other GPU waits. At the cascade’s 6.2 failures/day, each minute removed from every recovery is worth about $2,100 per day, so the spare pays for itself once it shortens each recovery by about 11 seconds, provided it is refilled before the next failure arrives some 3.9 hours later. The same node beside an eight-GPU job, whose GPU-only MTBF in table 1 is 6,250 hours (260 days), would sit idle far longer than it could ever save. Scale reverses the verdict on the same hardware, and three foundational principles distill how that shift guides design across the rest of this chapter.

Systems Perspective 7.2: Three rules of failure at scale
  1. At scale, failures are continuous, not exceptional. A 10,000-GPU cluster experiences a failure every 3.9 hours on average. Systems must be designed expecting failure as normal operation.
  2. Checkpoint intervals have an optimum. The Young-Daly interval (developed in section 7.8.1) sets checkpoint frequency from write time and system MTBF under its stated assumptions, and it shrinks only with the square root of a falling MTBF.
  3. Training and serving have fundamentally different fault tolerance requirements. A long training job can budget minutes for recovery while preserving accumulated progress; an interactive serving request may have only milliseconds left before its deadline. Those budgets motivate checkpoint recovery for training and ready alternate capacity for serving.

These three rules organize the rest of the chapter: rules 1 and 2 govern long-running training jobs that must protect accumulated progress, rule 3 governs low-latency serving fleets that must protect live requests, and observability and fault injection verify that both recovery paths work under fire. Training resilience begins with the durable state that every restart or resize falls back on.

Self-Check: Question

  1. The expected cost of a training run is modeled as base compute, plus expected failures times the cost per failure, plus the cost of fault-tolerance mechanisms. Under this model, when does one more unit of fault-tolerance investment lower the total expected cost?

    1. Whenever it reduces the expected number of failures, regardless of what it costs.
    2. Whenever the cluster is large enough to fail several times per day.
    3. While the reduction in expected failure cost it buys exceeds its own marginal cost.
    4. Whenever total fault-tolerance spending stays below the base compute cost of the run.

  2. A 175B-parameter language model trains on 10,000 GPUs at about $2 per GPU-hour, and a ViT-Large model trains for two days on a much smaller allocation. Both checkpoint once a day. Why can one failure cost the language model run roughly $0.5M of compute but the vision run only tens of thousands of dollars?

    1. Each GPU in the language model run fails more often, so every failure destroys more work.
    2. Lost progress is priced by what the allocation costs per hour and how much state must be recovered, so up to a day of rework on a 10,000-GPU allocation costs about $0.5M while a smaller allocation loses far less per day.
    3. Vision checkpoints are larger than language model checkpoints, which shortens vision recovery.
    4. Vision runs carry no state beyond their weights, so they can resume from any data position at no cost.

  3. On the 10,000-GPU cluster, about 6.2 failures per day each stall every GPU during recovery, at about $2 per GPU-hour. An idle eight-GPU hot-spare node costs about $384 per day. Estimate how much recovery time the spare must save per failure to pay for itself, and state the condition attached to that estimate.

  4. True or False: A hot-spare node that pays for itself beside a 10,000-GPU training cluster is also a sound investment beside an eight-GPU job, because the node and its daily cost are identical.

See Answers →

7.8 Checkpointing and Progress Preservation

When a top-of-rack switch dies two weeks into the training run of a 175-billion-parameter model, the cluster loses 64 GPUs instantly. Because a large cluster experiences such interruptions routinely (section 7.1), it cannot restart multi-week jobs from scratch; training fault tolerance must preserve state, detect and classify the failure, and resume or resize the worker group. Checkpointing supplies the first of those three obligations, and every detection, restart, and elastic-resizing path later in the chapter depends on the durable snapshot it produces. The first design question is what that snapshot must contain to make a restart valid.

Definition 7.1: Checkpointing

Checkpointing is the periodic serialization of the complete training state (parameters, optimizer state, data loader position, and random state) to persistent storage at a recorded training step.

  1. Significance: It minimizes lost work after a system failure by trading checkpoint I/O against rework. Writing too often wastes accelerator time on storage traffic; writing too rarely risks replaying many completed steps after a failure. The Young-Daly interval developed in this section chooses the cadence that balances write cost against expected lost work.
  2. Distinction: A resumable checkpoint captures a consistent execution state, including optimizer state, random-number generator state, data position, and learning rate schedules. It can use full or incremental storage; bitwise replay additionally requires compatible deterministic execution.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that checkpointing is “just writing to disk.” For large models it is a storage-system stress test, because the simultaneous write from thousands of GPUs can trigger a checkpoint storm (definition 4.5) that saturates the shared network and storage bandwidth \((\Bandwidth)\).

A checkpoint is useful only if it resumes the recorded step consistently. In distributed training, every shard must belong to the same recoverable state; a collection of files from different steps does not preserve progress. For the interval model, assume that writing a checkpoint completes durably before it becomes available for recovery and that a failure discards the work performed since the last completed checkpoint.

Two recovery objectives turn those obligations into a contract. The recovery point objective (RPO) bounds acceptable lost progress, so checkpoint age and replication lag must fit within it. The recovery time objective (RTO) bounds the delay until useful work resumes, which covers detection, resource replacement, restore, and warmup. A 20-minute checkpoint interval cannot promise an RPO below 20 minutes without a more frequent durable log or replica, and asynchronous flushing can leave the last durable generation older still. A five-minute RTO is impossible if checkpoint reload alone takes eight minutes. These objectives apply to a stated fault model. Corruption discovered after several saves can require rollback beyond the newest durable checkpoint. The Young-Daly interval derived next minimizes expected waste; RPO and RTO impose service constraints that the expected-value optimum must still satisfy (Beyer et al. 2016).

7.8.1 The Young-Daly checkpoint interval

When failures are expected during a job, the key engineering decision is how often to write that state, because every save costs I/O time and every unsaved step is work a failure can erase. The Young-Daly formula12 (principle 13) gives a first-order estimate of that balance through a square-root rule:

12 Young-Daly formula: Young (1974) derived the first-order optimal checkpoint interval, and Daly (2006) independently refined it with tighter second-order bounds.

Young, John W. 1974. “A First Order Approximation to the Optimum Checkpoint Interval.” Communications of the ACM 17 (9): 530–31. https://doi.org/10.1145/361147.361115.
Daly, J. T. 2006. “A Higher Order Estimate of the Optimum Checkpoint Interval for Restart Dumps.” Future Generation Computer Systems 22 (3): 303–12. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.future.2004.11.016.

\[ \OptimalCheckpointInterval = \sqrt{2 \cdot \CheckpointWriteTime \cdot \SystemMtbf} \tag{7}\]

\(\CheckpointWriteTime\) in distributed ML training is not the time to flush a small process state; it includes transferring model weights and optimizer state to durable storage. The reference format here stores BF16 weights, separate FP32 master weights, and two FP32 moment tensors per parameter for Adam. A single resumable checkpoint for a 70B-parameter model therefore approaches a terabyte at 14 bytes per parameter; for the later 175B-parameter running example, it exceeds two terabytes. Of those 14 bytes per parameter, the optimizer state13 dominates the payload, exactly as in the storage sizing of the same resumable checkpoint in Checkpoint Storage. The optimizer state rather than the model weights therefore sets most of \(\CheckpointWriteTime\). Reducing \(\CheckpointWriteTime\) requires a smaller payload or higher sustained write bandwidth, and \(\CheckpointWriteTime\) is the central constraint that determines how tightly the checkpoint interval can be set before I/O overhead displaces productive training compute.

13 Adam optimizer state: Adam’s per-parameter first-moment (\(\AdamFirstMoment\)) and second-moment (\(\AdamSecondMoment\)) estimates are two FP32 values, 8 of the 14 bytes per parameter in the resumable checkpoint; with the FP32 master weights, optimizer-side state accounts for all but the 2 bytes of BF16 weights. For a 175B parameter model, the two moments alone reach 1.4 TB, which typically dominates checkpoint size and recovery time, making optimizer state the primary bottleneck in checkpoint I/O design.

Optimizer state, not weights, dominates checkpoint size.

To see the balance, consider an interval between saves. Doubling that interval approximately halves the checkpoint writes per unit of training, but a failure then loses twice as much work on average. Under the constant-rate, rare-failure approximation, failure occurs approximately uniformly within an interval, so the average lost work is half the interval. When write time is small relative to the interval, the wasted fraction from saving and replaying work is approximately the sum below. Detection, allocation, and restoration time require a separate recovery budget.

\[ \CheckpointWasteFraction(\CheckpointInterval) \approx \frac{\CheckpointWriteTime}{\CheckpointInterval} + \frac{\CheckpointInterval}{2 \cdot \SystemMtbf} \tag{8}\]

Setting the first derivative to zero (\(\frac{d\CheckpointWasteFraction}{d\CheckpointInterval} = -\frac{\CheckpointWriteTime}{\CheckpointInterval^2} + \frac{1}{2 \cdot \SystemMtbf} = 0\)) directly yields the square-root minimum of equation 7. As clusters grow, system MTBF drops, so the optimal interval shrinks, demanding higher-bandwidth storage to keep \(\CheckpointWriteTime\) small before checkpoint overhead consumes the cluster’s compute capacity. Because the relationship is a square root, halving MTBF tightens the interval by only \(\sqrt{2}\approx 1.4\times\), so doubling cluster size does not demand doubling checkpoint I/O bandwidth when checkpoint size and the other assumptions stay fixed. Substituting the optimum back into equation 8 gives a minimum waste of \(\sqrt{2\CheckpointWriteTime/\SystemMtbf}\). Because \(\SystemMtbf\) falls as \(1/\DeviceCount\) under independent failures, that optimized overhead grows as \(\sqrt{\DeviceCount}\), and doubling the cluster raises it by a factor of \(\sqrt{2}\) even though the interval itself adapts.

↩︎ The per-tier storage bandwidths that bound how quickly a checkpoint can be written are established in The ML Storage Hierarchy.

7.8.2 Checkpoint interval from failure analysis

The Young-Daly interval of equation 7 needs two inputs, the system MTBF and the write time \(\CheckpointWriteTime\), and the chapter’s 10,000-GPU cluster supplies both as computed values. The worked cascade (section 7.2.2) put full-system MTBF, including PCIe and network failures (the redundant power supplies add no term), at 3.87 hours. For the write time, the 175B running example’s 2.45 TB resumable checkpoint drains to shared storage at the sustained aggregate write bandwidth this chapter assumes for the cluster, 100 GB/s, which takes 24.5 s. Substituting these into the Young-Daly formula gives a candidate interval for this cluster, which must still satisfy its RPO and RTO.

Napkin Math 7.2: The Young-Daly optimal interval
Problem: A 10,000-GPU cluster has an MTBF of 3.87 hours. A full model checkpoint takes 24.5 s to write. What is the optimal checkpoint frequency?

Math: Apply the Young-Daly interval of equation 7 (principle 13).

  1. Convert to common units: \(\SystemMtbf =\) 3.87 hours \(\times\) 3600 s/hour = 13932 s.
  2. Substitute and solve: With \(\CheckpointWriteTime =\) 24.5 s and \(\SystemMtbf =\) 13932 s, the formula gives \(\OptimalCheckpointInterval \approx\) 826.2 s, or about 13.8 min.

Systems insight: At this interval, the checkpoint overhead (time spent saving plus time spent recomputing) is minimized to approximately 5.9 percent. Checkpointing every hour instead would lengthen the expected rework after each failure, wasting about 13.6 percent of the cluster’s capacity.

The result connects measured or estimated interruption rates to checkpoint policy. At this interval, saving and expected replay together consume approximately 5.9 percent of training time in the first-order model, half from writes and half from replay, because at the square-root optimum the two terms of equation 8 are equal. Plotting those two terms for this cluster shows how forgiving the optimum is (figure 11). Write overhead falls hyperbolically and expected rework climbs linearly, so their sum has a shallow floor around 14 min. An interval slightly off the optimum costs little, while one several times too short or too long costs far more, which is why a rough MTBF estimate still yields a usable schedule. How far the estimate itself can be trusted depends on the simplifying assumptions behind it.

Systems Perspective 7.3: Young-Daly formula assumptions
The first-order Young-Daly formula provides valuable intuition but rests on assumptions that may not hold when write times are non-negligible relative to MTBF; the renewal model derived in Checkpoint Intervals Beyond the First-Order Approximation shows how finite checkpoint time changes the optimum. The assumptions are:

  • Exponentially distributed failures: Assumes a constant failure rate. Real hardware follows the bathtub curve, with higher rates during burn-in and wear-out phases.
  • Deterministic checkpoint time: Assumes \(\CheckpointWriteTime\) is constant. In practice, checkpoint time varies from save to save with storage contention, network congestion, and memory pressure.
  • Recovery cost is independent of the chosen interval: A fixed per-failure recovery cost does not change the first-order minimizing interval. Recovery can take longer than checkpoint writing because it also includes job scheduling delays, topology reconstruction, and warmup; it still belongs in the total-waste budget.
  • Aggregate interruption process: Independent constant-rate failure sources can contribute to one job-interruption rate. Shared power, cooling, or switch failures require a model of incident frequency and recovery cost; adding per-device rates alone misses those common causes.
  • Infinite timeline: Optimizes for long training runs. Short runs, where total time is comparable to MTBF, require different analysis.

When assumptions are violated, the optimal interval may shift significantly, but restart overhead should not be treated as if it were paid at every checkpoint. In the first-order lost-time model, restart time adds a per-failure recovery term to total expected waste; it does not replace \(\CheckpointWriteTime\) inside equation 7 unless a recovery-aware Daly-style model is explicitly derived and calibrated.

Along an execution timeline, a training run alternates productive compute intervals (\(\CheckpointInterval\)) with synchronous save barriers (\(\CheckpointWriteTime\)), and a failure charges two distinct penalties (figure 12). Uncheckpointed progress is discarded as lost rework, and the restart latency (\(\FailureRecoveryTime\)) of job scheduling, checkpoint loading, and pipeline warmup follows before useful work resumes. Recovery latency therefore matters as much as write duration. Restart adds scheduling and warmup to reading the saved state back, so total restart time can exceed the checkpoint write time. It belongs in the total-waste and service-level objective (SLO) budgets as a per-failure term (perspective 7.3) rather than inside the per-checkpoint write term.

7.8.3 Checkpoint overhead analysis

The interval calculation chooses when the system preserves state; the write path decides what each save costs while training waits. Equation 9 generalizes the save-side term of equation 8 to include any pause beyond the write itself:

\[ \CheckpointOverheadFraction= \frac{\CheckpointWriteTime}{\CheckpointInterval} + \frac{\CheckpointExtraPauseTime}{\CheckpointInterval} \tag{9}\]

Here, \(\CheckpointOverheadFraction\) is the dimensionless fraction of time lost to checkpointing, \(\CheckpointWriteTime\) is checkpoint write time, \(\CheckpointInterval\) is the checkpoint interval, and \(\CheckpointExtraPauseTime\) is any training pause beyond the checkpoint write itself. This includes memory allocation, coordination, and serialization. Total first-order waste is then \(\CheckpointWasteFraction(\CheckpointInterval) \approx \CheckpointOverheadFraction+ \CheckpointInterval/(2 \cdot \SystemMtbf)\), so checkpoint overhead in this section means the save side alone, with expected rework counted separately. How large \(\CheckpointOverheadFraction\) becomes depends first on whether training waits for durable storage.

Synchronous vs. asynchronous checkpointing

Synchronous checkpointing resumes training only after every worker’s portion reaches durable storage. Asynchronous checkpointing instead pauses the accelerators only long enough to copy the weights and optimizer tensors from GPU memory into host memory or local staging, then flushes that staged copy to remote storage in the background while training continues. The brief device-to-host copy is necessary because the next optimizer step updates the live GPU tensors in place, which would otherwise overwrite state that is still being serialized. Synchronous and asynchronous checkpoint writes develops the write path behind both approaches. For recovery, blocking alone does not establish consistency, so the protocol must ensure that every shard belongs to the same recoverable training state. Recovery can use an asynchronous generation only after all required shards reach the storage tier that survives the modeled failure and the coordinator publishes completion. If a failure strikes while the background flush is still in flight, recovery must fall back to the previous committed generation.

A synchronous save pauses every accelerator in the job for the full remote write, and Checkpoint Storage prices that exposed pause on a slow shared tier. Asynchronous capture shrinks the exposed pause to the local staging copy and moves the remote flush off the critical path, which is why production systems stage checkpoints rather than block on shared storage. Write time also varies widely across model types, because larger models require longer writes but also earn longer optimal intervals (table 6).

The Archetype A row in table 6 uses the same 14-byte-per-parameter resumable checkpoint (2.45 TB) as the rest of the chapter. Its interval calculation uses the GPU-only cluster MTBF rather than the shorter full-system MTBF of the worked cascade, so the square-root rule gives a longer interval than the Young-Daly notebook. The table keeps the GPU-only basis because its purpose is comparison across model types. Switching every row to the full-system MTBF would shorten each interval, and raise each overhead, by the same square-root factor, leaving the ranking across rows unchanged. The ResNet-50 and BERT-Large rows count only FP32 weights, so their write times and resulting intervals are lower bounds for a resumable checkpoint. Their intervals of seconds describe the balance for that smaller payload; selecting a recovery cadence requires including optimizer, schedule, and input-stream state. Small-state jobs rarely run on a cluster this large, and their actual storage bandwidth and complete recovery state determine whether saves every few minutes or every epoch have negligible overhead. The large-state rows show why interval selection becomes consequential when each save moves hundreds of gigabytes or terabytes.

These per-model overheads assume the write path is uncontended. When thousands of workers save at the same step, their combined burst becomes the checkpoint storm that Checkpoint Storage defines and sizes; for recovery, the burst matters because a stalled write delays the generation that a failure would fall back on. Staging hides the write from training, yet a snapshot held only on local staging does not survive loss of its node, so where each copy lives decides which failures the saved state outlasts and how quickly it returns.

7.8.4 Checkpoint storage and recovery

Writing a checkpoint to node-local NVMe makes the save fast, but if the node itself fails, that local snapshot vanishes along with the hardware it was meant to recover. Writing only to a durable remote filesystem or object store survives the node failure, but pulling multi-terabyte state back across shared storage links slows every recovery. Recovery mechanisms therefore pair tiers from the storage hierarchy. Local NVMe provides fast staging and survivor reads, distributed filesystems provide durability across node failures, and object storage provides long-term retention. Together, the tiers let the saved state both outlast the fault and reload within the recovery budget.

↩︎ Tiered checkpoint storage designs and asynchronous write pipelines are established in Checkpoint Storage.

For fault tolerance, checkpoint placement determines which failures the saved state survives, while the restore path determines how quickly that surviving state becomes useful. On the 128-node Archetype A reference cluster, each node holds about 19.1 GB of the 2.45 TB checkpoint for the 175B running example. Reading that share from local NVMe at 5 GB/s takes about 3.8 s, with every node reading in parallel, while pulling the full checkpoint back through shared storage at 100 GB/s aggregate takes about 24.5 s. The local path is faster because it multiplies drive bandwidth by node count, but a sole copy on a lost node cannot restore that node’s shard. The replacement node must read its share from a copy outside the failed domain, such as a distributed filesystem or object store, whose recovery time is set by sustained aggregate reads, network capacity, and deserialization. A sound placement therefore keeps a fast local copy for the survivors and a durable remote copy for whatever the failure took away, and these rates must still be measured under the job’s own load.

7.8.5 Checkpoint coordination patterns

If workers in a sharded training job save their local shards at different steps without coordinating, rolling one failed worker back to its last save invalidates every peer that already consumed gradients or activations from its later steps. Rollbacks then cascade backward through the run in the classic domino effect (Elnozahy et al. 2002). Avoiding that cascade requires choosing how workers coordinate across two orthogonal dimensions. Workers can vary in whether they synchronize to capture a consistent cut (coordinated versus uncoordinated checkpointing). They can also vary in whether they funnel state through one writer or persist shards in parallel (centralized versus distributed checkpointing). Because distributed ML workers exchange state at every step, production training relies on coordinated checkpointing. In this scheme, workers synchronize at step boundaries or through an explicit two-phase commit protocol, so that all saved shards belong to a single globally consistent recovery line. Once coordination guarantees consistency, the remaining architectural decision is how those shards reach persistent storage: whether workers funnel their state through a single coordinator or write their partitions directly in parallel.

Elnozahy, Elmootazbellah N., Lorenzo Alvisi, Yi-Min Wang, and David B. Johnson. 2002. “A Survey of Rollback-Recovery Protocols in Message-Passing Systems.” ACM Computing Surveys 34 (3): 375–408. https://doi.org/10.1145/568522.568525.

Centralized checkpointing

In centralized checkpointing, workers send their state to a coordinator process that assembles and writes the complete checkpoint. This approach simplifies checkpoint management and can produce self-contained checkpoint files, but all state crosses the coordinator’s network links, and coordinator failure interrupts the save. A streaming writer can avoid buffering the entire checkpoint, although it cannot remove the ingress bottleneck. That bottleneck is structural. The coordinator receives through one node’s network links, so its write time grows with total state volume, whereas independent writers each move only their own share of the state through their own links. The gap between the two patterns therefore widens in proportion to the number of writers. For the 175B running example, the 128 nodes of the reference cluster would funnel 2.45 TB through one node’s links instead of 19.1 GB through each. The pattern is viable while that one path meets the checkpoint budget; state volume and sustained bandwidth determine the limit more directly than a fixed worker count. Centralized checkpointing is therefore attractive when operational simplicity matters more than aggregate bandwidth, whereas distributed and sharded approaches become necessary once the checkpoint itself is a fleet-scale object.

Distributed checkpointing

Distributed checkpointing eliminates this coordinator funnel by having each worker persist its own shard directly. Figure 13 contrasts the dataflow topologies: the centralized pattern concentrates traffic at the coordinator’s ingress, while the distributed pattern writes in parallel to local NVMe and drains the shards to remote object storage in the background. With fixed per-worker shard sizes and sufficient independent staging bandwidth, adding workers need not lengthen the local write pause. Shared drain capacity and completion coordination can still limit scaling. A coordinator signals when to checkpoint and confirms completion, but state flows directly from workers to storage without aggregation.

A synchronous version of the coordination protocol proceeds in six steps:

  1. Coordinator broadcasts checkpoint request with checkpoint ID
  2. Each worker reaches a consistent state (barrier synchronization)
  3. Each worker writes an immutable shard to a generation-specific staging prefix and records its byte length and cryptographic digest
  4. Each worker confirms durable write completion to the coordinator
  5. The coordinator writes an immutable manifest after all confirmations; the manifest names the training step, topology, model and optimizer formats, data-loader position, random state, and the length and digest of every shard, then publishes a commit marker or atomic pointer to that manifest
  6. Coordinator broadcasts checkpoint complete, training resumes

The idealized protocol assumes step 2 completes quickly. The barrier, however, waits for the slowest of thousands of arrivals, so a single slow worker can make it dominate the checkpoint pause even when the storage path has ample bandwidth. Measuring arrival skew separately from copying and writing reveals whether coordination or data movement limits the save.

The manifest, not the presence of a directory or a plausible set of shard names, defines a committed checkpoint generation. Restore accepts only a published manifest whose shards all match their recorded lengths and digests; staged shards without that final publication remain unreachable and can be garbage collected. This rule prevents a partially overwritten latest directory from masquerading as recoverable state and makes generation rollback explicit. PyTorch Distributed Checkpoint follows this ordering, with storage writers persisting each rank’s data before the coordinator’s finish() operation writes the metadata and marks the checkpoint successful, while the storage backend supplies the publication semantics (PyTorch Contributors 2026a). The set of per-worker states a manifest names is a cut, and the cut is consistent when all of those states could have existed together at one moment of a valid execution. In a consistent cut, no shard reflects an update another shard has not yet seen. That consistency cut determines whether the system can claim strict, bounded-asynchronous, or eventual consistency.

PyTorch Contributors. 2026a. Distributed Checkpoint: Torch.distributed.checkpoint.

Systems Perspective 7.4: Checkpoint consistency models
Strict synchronous: All workers checkpoint at exactly the same training step. Provides strongest consistency but highest overhead from barrier synchronization.

Bounded asynchronous: Workers may progress at different rates while the checkpoint manager tracks which snapshots can form a consistent recovery cut. Bounding step skew alone is insufficient, because the system must retain compatible shard versions or enough replay information to reconstruct that cut. Asynchronous flushing can also preserve a single synchronized snapshot while its writes complete in the background, avoiding training stalls without relaxing recovery consistency.

Eventual consistency: Workers save independently and recovery attempts to reconcile their versions. A usable design must retain enough compatible versions or replay information to reconstruct a valid state; arbitrary recent shards do not necessarily form a recoverable generation. Reduced coordination at save time therefore transfers work and storage requirements to recovery.

Steps 3 through 5 are two-phase commit,14 the classic distributed-systems protocol for making one decision atomic across many participants (Gray 1978), applied to a checkpoint. Steps 3 and 4 form its prepare phase, in which each worker stages its shard and reports success, and step 5 is its commit phase, in which the coordinator durably records the decision by publishing the manifest. The manifest publication rule inherits the protocol’s guarantee and its known weakness. If the coordinator crashes after workers prepare but before it records a decision, the system must not expose those shards as a valid checkpoint, and the prepared participants remain blocked until the original or a replacement coordinator recovers the durable decision. Uncommitted staged shards can be garbage collected only after recovery establishes that no commit decision exists. Atomic publication also does not by itself make the published shards consistent: synchronous gradient updates naturally create step boundaries where all workers have applied the same updates, whereas asynchronous training and pipeline parallelism require explicit coordination to define a consistent cut.

14 Two-phase commit (2PC): Formalized by Gray (1978), 2PC ensures all participants either commit or abort atomically. A timeout can trigger coordinator recovery, but it cannot by itself authorize unilateral rollback.

Gray, J. N. 1978. “Notes on Data Base Operating Systems.” In Operating Systems: An Advanced Course. Springer Berlin Heidelberg. https://doi.org/10.1007/3-540-08755-9_9.

Sharded checkpointing

Modern distributed training frameworks partition model state across workers using techniques like ZeRO and FSDP, which shard parameters, gradients, and optimizer state instead of replicating them on every data-parallel worker. In these configurations, no single worker holds complete model state. Each worker holds only its assigned parameter shard plus corresponding optimizer state.

↩︎ How ZeRO and FSDP partition training state across data-parallel workers is established in Memory-efficient data parallelism with ZeRO and FSDP.

15 Sharded checkpointing: Each worker saves only its local partition rather than gathering state to a single writer, parallelizing I/O across all nodes. The trade-off is that recovery requires every shard to be available and consistent, making the checkpoint protocol more complex. Losing one storage device invalidates the generation only if a required shard cannot be recovered from another copy or sufficient storage redundancy.

Rajbhandari, Samyam, Jeff Rasley, Olatunji Ruwase, and Yuxiong He. 2020. “ZeRO: Memory Optimizations Toward Training Trillion Parameter Models.” SC20: International Conference for High Performance Computing, Networking, Storage and Analysis, 1–16. https://doi.org/10.1109/sc41405.2020.00024.

Sharded checkpointing15 (Rajbhandari et al. 2020) uses this distribution. Each worker writes only its shard, which divides the per-worker write volume by the number of shards. Recovery loads shards and redistributes state to workers based on the recovery configuration.

Dividing state across the fleet keeps per-worker saves small even for multi-terabyte models. A 175B parameter model with a 2.45 TB checkpoint distributed across 1,024 workers requires each worker to write only 2.4 GB, achievable in seconds with local NVMe storage. When elastic scaling or hardware replacement changes the worker count between save and restore, shard redistribution must remap the saved partitions to the new worker configuration, which is why frameworks need flexible resharding support to recover across topology changes. Yet possessing a valid checkpoint is only half the recovery path. Sharding distributes the write work and staging controls the shared I/O burst, but the system must still detect the failure, trigger restoration, and coordinate the distributed shards before training can resume. The speed of detection and recovery therefore governs the cost of the interruption.

Checkpoint 7.4: Reasoning about checkpoint overhead

Verify your understanding of how failure rate and checkpoint cost set the optimal checkpoint interval:

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team checkpoints every 30 minutes with asynchronous flushing. It promises a recovery point objective (RPO) of 15 minutes and a recovery time objective (RTO) of 10 minutes, and reloading the checkpoint alone takes 12 minutes. Which assessment is correct?

    1. Both promises hold, because the Young-Daly interval already minimizes expected waste.
    2. The RPO holds because asynchronous flushing removes the write from the critical path, but the RTO fails.
    3. The RTO holds because detection and warmup overlap with checkpoint reload, but the RPO fails.
    4. Both promises fail, because a 30-minute interval cannot bound lost progress below 30 minutes without a more frequent durable log or replica, and a 12-minute reload alone exceeds the 10-minute RTO before detection, replacement, and warmup are counted.

  2. A training cluster quadruples in size from 1,000 GPUs to 4,000 GPUs, reducing its system MTBF by a factor of 4. Assuming checkpoint write time \(T_{\text{write}}\) remains constant, calculate by what factor the Young-Daly optimal checkpoint interval \(\tau_{\text{opt}}\) changes.

  3. How does sharded checkpointing (such as in ZeRO-3 or PyTorch FSDP) differ from centralized checkpointing in terms of I/O parallelism and recovery requirements?

    1. Sharded checkpointing requires every worker to broadcast its state to all peers before writing, doubling network communication overhead.
    2. Sharded checkpointing writes a single monolithic file from Rank 0, minimizing storage metadata operations.
    3. Each worker writes only its locally partitioned parameter and optimizer shard directly to storage in parallel, aggregating storage fabric bandwidth but requiring all shards to be present and consistent during recovery.
    4. Sharded checkpointing stores only model weights, discarding optimizer state to achieve sub-second write times.

  4. Explain the memory composition breakdown of checkpoint files for large language models trained with mixed-precision AdamW, detailing why optimizer states dominate storage requirements.

  5. Sequence the 6 steps of a standard distributed sharded checkpoint coordination protocol in chronological order:

  1. Coordinator confirms all writes and commits checkpoint metadata
  2. Each worker writes its local state shard directly to persistent storage
  3. Coordinator broadcasts checkpoint completion signal, allowing next barrier clearance
  4. Coordinator broadcasts checkpoint initiation request with a unique checkpoint ID
  5. Each worker reaches a consistent execution state via barrier synchronization
  6. Each worker reports shard write completion back to the coordinator

  1. What is the primary advantage of adopting a bounded asynchronous checkpoint consistency model over a strict synchronous checkpoint model in a 10,000-GPU cluster?

    1. It can reduce barrier waiting by letting workers progress within a bounded step window while retaining compatible shard versions or replay information to construct a consistent recovery cut.
    2. It guarantees that all workers write their state at the exact same nanosecond without requiring any communication with a coordinator.
    3. It eliminates the need for persistent storage by maintaining checkpoints entirely in L2 cache memory.
    4. It ensures that lost computation after a hardware crash is always zero steps.

See Answers →

7.9 Failure Detection and Recovery

A GPU silently hangs, dropping its utilization to zero while its peer GPUs wait indefinitely at an AllReduce barrier. On the chapter’s 10,000-GPU cluster, every minute the system takes to notice this hung rank and reboot the node costs about $330 in idle compute. Checkpoints preserve state, but the recovery process itself determines how much compute a failure wastes. In the accounting of the Fleet Law (equation 1.3), that waiting belongs to the synchronization term \(\SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount)\). Every surviving rank idles at the barrier until the group is whole again, so detection, restart, and reload add directly to step time averaged over the run. The goodput accounting of Accounting boundaries charges the same time to runtime goodput.

Equation 10 decomposes recovery time into four primary components:

\[ \FailureRecoveryTime= \FailureDetectionTime+ \JobRestartTime+ \CheckpointLoadTime+ \RecoveryWarmupTime \tag{10}\]

where \(\FailureDetectionTime\) is the time between the physical hardware fault and system classification as a failure. The term \(\JobRestartTime\) is the latency required for the job scheduler to allocate resources and launch replacement processes. The term \(\CheckpointLoadTime\) is the I/O duration to read checkpoint state from distributed storage into GPU memory. The term \(\RecoveryWarmupTime\) is the interval needed to refill data pipelines, compile just-in-time (JIT) kernels, and stabilize training throughput. Each component presents distinct optimization opportunities, and the dominant term varies by cluster configuration. Understanding this decomposition enables targeted investment in the bottleneck rather than uniform improvement across all components.

7.9.1 Failure detection mechanisms

Detection latency climbs from seconds (crash) to hours (silent corruption).

To the rest of the cluster, a dead rank and a rank busy flushing a multi-gigabyte checkpoint shard both look silent. Every failure detector must therefore balance two expensive mistakes: setting timeouts too short kills healthy workers during transient network or storage stalls, while setting them too long leaves thousands of peer GPUs idling at a collective barrier. Heartbeat monitoring is the first detection layer and usually sets the floor on \(\FailureDetectionTime\). Each GPU rank periodically signals liveness to a coordinator, and if no heartbeat arrives within the timeout period \(\HandoverTimeout\), the coordinator declares the worker failed.

For a heartbeat interval of \(\HeartbeatInterval\) seconds and network-delay standard deviation \(\NetworkDelayStandardDeviation\) in seconds, equation 11 defines the timeout heuristic:

\[ \HandoverTimeout = \HeartbeatInterval+ \TimeoutSafetyMultiplier\NetworkDelayStandardDeviation \tag{11}\]

Here, \(\TimeoutSafetyMultiplier\) is a dimensionless safety multiplier; values from three to five illustrate a tuning range rather than a guaranteed false positive rate. The heuristic treats the normal heartbeat delivery delay as small relative to the heartbeat interval. A deployment with substantial baseline delay or a heavy delay tail must account for those measurements as well as \(\NetworkDelayStandardDeviation\). Tuning \(\HandoverTimeout\) too tightly causes legitimate checkpoint writes or long collectives to trigger false failure declarations; tuning it too loosely extends the window during which other ranks wait for the missing contribution.

Collective communication timeouts provide a second detection layer. During synchronous training, a collective required by the next step remains an incomplete critical-path dependency if a required rank disappears. AllReduce needs every rank’s contribution before it can produce the reduced result; the progress and failure behavior of other collectives depends on the operation and runtime. Transport and process-group timeouts bound how long the job waits before declaring the operation failed and entering recovery. NCCL16 provides configurable transport and reliability, availability, and serviceability (RAS) timeout parameters for this purpose, while higher-level frameworks may impose process-group operation timeouts. These settings are often set conservatively to avoid crashing jobs during legitimate periods of slow communication, which unfortunately extends \(\FailureDetectionTime\).

16 NCCL timeout: NVIDIA’s collective communication library exposes transport and RAS timeout controls such as NCCL_IB_TIMEOUT and NCCL_RAS_TIMEOUT_FACTOR (NVIDIA 2026). Higher-level launch and distributed-training frameworks can add their own restart and operation-timeout behavior (PyTorch Contributors 2026c). Aggressive timeout settings accelerate failure detection but risk false positives during legitimate long collective operations on large models, forcing a trade-off between detection latency and training stability that has no universal optimum.

NVIDIA. 2026. NCCL User Guide.
PyTorch Contributors. 2026c. Torchrun (Elastic Launch). PyTorch documentation.

Container orchestration health checks provide a third layer. Kubernetes, the container orchestration system, offers liveness probes (verifying that a process still responds and makes progress) and readiness probes (verifying that a process is ready to handle requests). Slurm, the batch scheduler common on HPC-style training clusters, instead runs a periodic node health-check program that can drain a node whose GPUs, links, or file systems fail their tests. Both operate independently of the training framework, catching failures that application-level heartbeats might miss; a liveness probe that tests forward progress rather than mere process existence can catch a process that is alive but deadlocked.

Loss spike detection exposes an anomaly, not its cause. Figure 14 contrasts an idealized healthy loss trend with gradient explosion, a premature plateau, and NaN divergence. Real stochastic training need not decrease monotonically, so a transient rise alone does not establish failure, and nonfinite values need not crash the process. Silent hardware corruption can produce an abrupt loss or gradient-norm spike, but so can a bad batch, an optimizer instability, a data-ordering defect, or a distributed-state mismatch. Robust systems therefore pause before the suspect update commits, preserve the input and worker evidence, and replay the step on known-good hardware. The system drains hardware only after that evidence implicates the device; otherwise, it repairs the data, optimizer, or synchronization path that produced the same symptom.

Table 7 pairs each signature, plus the silent hang that a loss curve cannot show, with a candidate cause and a first response. The pairing guides investigation without treating the curve as a unique diagnosis, and the operational question that follows is how long detection actually takes once these monitoring layers interact.

Systems Perspective 7.5: Realistic failure detection latencies

Failure detection includes more than the configured heartbeat timeout when the policy requires verification before recovery. The ranges in table 8 illustrate planning scenarios, not universal detection guarantees. Aggressive timeouts risk false positives (killing healthy-but-slow workers), while conservative timeouts delay real failure detection. A multi-stage policy can use an initial timeout to trigger investigation and a later threshold to trigger recovery. Its actual latency must be measured under the workload and failure conditions it is intended to handle.

7.9.2 Recovery procedures

Once a failure is classified, a cold-restart recovery path executes a coordinated sequence to restore consistency. The warm-restart alternative developed next preserves surviving processes when their state can be trusted.

  1. Job termination: A SIGTERM is broadcast to all surviving workers. This cold-restart path tears down the affected training group so that no survivor continues using the old membership or partially completed collective state.
  2. Resource reclamation: The scheduler marks the failed node as “draining” to prevent immediate rescheduling and requests a replacement from the spare pool.
  3. Job restart: New containers are launched (from cache if available), and the training binary is re-initialized on all nodes.
  4. Checkpoint loading: Each worker reads the state assigned to it from the distributed filesystem. When membership or parallelism changes, recovery remaps the saved shards to the new partitions.
  5. State synchronization: Ranks handshake to establish a new communicator (for example, ncclCommInitRank), and workers verify they are all at the same training step.
  6. Training resumption: The data loader restores its recorded position and sampling state, and the training loop resumes from the checkpoint step. A batch index alone suffices only when it reconstructs the same logical input stream.

Automatic recovery systems perform these steps without human intervention. Modern training frameworks integrate with cluster managers to automate parts of the sequence. DeepSpeed documents save/load routines for model and ZeRO optimizer checkpoints, which provide the durable state needed after failure (DeepSpeed Developers 2026a). PyTorch’s torchrun elastic launch documents failure and membership-change behavior through its rendezvous mechanism,17 the coordinator-backed protocol by which surviving workers agree on membership and ranks after a restart (PyTorch Contributors 2026c, 2026b).

17 Rendezvous: From French “rendez-vous” (present yourselves), this coordination protocol lets restarted workers discover one another and establish a new group before training resumes. Recovery waits for the membership policy to be satisfied, so slow startup, delayed communication, and missing workers can extend this phase. Heterogeneity affects those delays but does not by itself imply a proportional latency law.

Recovery validation tests whether the restored job behaves consistently with its recorded state. Parameter shapes, dtypes, and shard identities must match the manifest. A short replay or validation workload then checks loss and gradient statistics under the expected numerical tolerance; different batches need not reproduce the last prefailure loss. Immediate divergence is evidence to investigate the checkpoint, data state, and execution configuration, not proof that the snapshot is corrupt. Falling back to an earlier validated generation is appropriate when the newest state cannot be trusted.

Recovery must also prevent the failed ownership epoch from returning. A coordinator grants each worker-group incarnation a monotonically increasing epoch (a membership counter, unrelated to a training epoch over the dataset) and associates membership with a renewable lease. Every state-changing write carries that epoch as a fencing token. Before replacement writes proceed, the recovery protocol advances the enforced epoch at each protected resource, which rejects writes carrying an older token (figure 15). The old worker then cannot publish a checkpoint or update job progress, even if it resumes after a long pause; rendezvous likewise admits only the current membership. A lease alone is insufficient because expiration changes the coordinator’s ownership decision, not the old worker’s ability to reach storage. Fencing enforces that decision at the resource boundary (Kleppmann 2016).

Kleppmann, Martin. 2016. Designing Data-Intensive Applications: The Big Ideas Behind Reliable, Scalable, and Maintainable Systems. O’Reilly Media.

Retries complete the recovery contract. An operation may be retried automatically only when it is idempotent or carries a stable operation identifier that lets the receiver deduplicate repeated effects. Each attempt inherits the caller’s absolute deadline rather than receiving a fresh timeout, and the system caps attempts with a retry budget shared across the request or recovery tree. Exponential backoff and jitter spread attempts in time, but they do not prevent overload if every layer retries independently. The budget therefore belongs at one retry-owning layer; exhausted budget returns a failure or invokes degradation instead of amplifying the original incident (Beyer et al. 2016; Nygard 2007).

Beyer, Betsy, Chris Jones, Jennifer Petoff, and Niall Richard Murphy. 2016. Site Reliability Engineering: How Google Runs Production Systems. O’Reilly Media.
Nygard, Michael T. 2007. Release It!: Design and Deploy Production-Ready Software. Pragmatic Bookshelf.

Napkin Math 7.3: The recovery time budget
Problem: Consider a 175B parameter model training on 1,024 GPUs. The checkpoint size is approximately 2.45 TB (BF16 weights, FP32 master weights, and Adam moments at 14 bytes per parameter). Estimate the cost of a single failure event.

Setup: The recovery budget \(\FailureRecoveryTime\) has four terms.

  • \(\FailureDetectionTime\): 60 s (conservative heartbeat timeout with verification retries).
  • \(\JobRestartTime\): 3 min (scheduler queue time + container launch + Python import overhead + NCCL initialization).
  • \(\CheckpointLoadTime\): 24.5 s (this is the aggregate-shared-storage read of the full 2.45 TB checkpoint at 100 GB/s aggregate). With sharded local-NVMe reads at 5 GB/s, one GPU’s 2.4 GB shard alone would load in under a second. The GPUs of a node share its drive, however, so the node’s 19.1 GB share takes about 3.8 s, and only on nodes that survived with their local copy).
  • \(\RecoveryWarmupTime\): 2 min (JIT kernel compilation, data pipeline buffer fill, TCP connection re-establishment).

Total: \(\FailureRecoveryTime= \FailureDetectionTime+ \JobRestartTime+ \CheckpointLoadTime+ \RecoveryWarmupTime\approx\) 6.4 min per failure event.

Impact: For this recovery path, \(\FailureRecoveryTime\) is the operational MTTR term in the steady-state availability relation (perspective 7.1). It is not the whole cost of a failure. Every failure also discards the work done since the last completed checkpoint, on average half the checkpoint interval (figure 12), and the Young-Daly waste of equation 8 already counts that rework, so the figures below price only the recovery term. Recovery time optimization matters more as clusters grow. The GPU-only baseline from table 1 compares the two sizes on a common basis and understates both failure counts. On that basis, a 1,024-GPU cluster has MTBF of 49 hours and experiences about 0.49 failures/day, losing about 3 minutes daily, a modest 0.2 percent overhead. However, a 10,000-GPU cluster has GPU-only MTBF of 5 hours and experiences about 4.8 failures/day, losing about 31 minutes daily (2.1 percent overhead). That is equivalent to wasting roughly 5,127 GPU-hours every day (the daily lost minutes multiplied across all 10,000 GPUs).

7.9.3 Warm restart vs. cold restart

The recovery procedure described in section 7.9.2 is a cold restart: every process in the affected job group exits, and the group reloads state from a valid checkpoint. This simplifies recovery by avoiding dependence on surviving in-memory state, but it can discard useful state. After one GPU fails in the 1,024-GPU reference job, every surviving worker may retain reusable values; an interrupted update or silent corruption, however, can make some of those values unsafe to keep.

A warm restart preserves the state of surviving workers. When a rank fails, surviving ranks detect the failure but do not exit. They enter a waiting state, preserving loaded model weights and optimizer states in GPU memory. The scheduler replaces only the failed node. The new node joins, loads its partition of the state from disk (or receives it via broadcast from a peer), and the communicator is rebuilt. The replacement and survivors must agree on the same committed training step before resuming. A shard restored from an older checkpoint cannot be combined with survivor shards that have advanced beyond it; the runtime must reconstruct the missing current state or roll the group back to a common recovery point. Training resumes only after that consistency check.

When the missing state can be reconstructed at the survivors’ current step, warm restart avoids a full-cluster reload and repeated warmup. For the 1,024 GPUs example, that fast path avoids rereading the full 2.45 TB checkpoint through shared storage, whose cold-path \(\CheckpointLoadTime\) is 24.5 s. The replacement still needs state transfer and initialization, and all ranks wait until it is ready. If consistency instead requires a rollback, the recovery budget must include the corresponding survivor-state restoration and lost work.

The trade-off is software complexity. Survivor-preserving warm restarts require specialized application and runtime support for dynamic membership changes without leaking CUDA memory, corrupting shared state, or deadlocking during communicator reconstruction. Common training frameworks restart the worker group or reload a checkpoint rather than preserve survivors (section 7.10.1.2). A deployment that implements a warm-restart fast path still needs cold restart as a safety net. The cold-restart range in table 9 brackets the 6.4 min budget computed above. The warm-restart range is a planning range for the fast path, which avoids the full-cluster reload and warmup; its actual duration depends on how quickly the replacement receives its state and the communicator is rebuilt.

7.9.4 Recovery automation pipeline

At the scale of 10,000+ GPUs, failures occur multiple times per day, so human operators cannot intervene manually on each incident. Recovery must be an autonomous control loop managed by the cluster control plane. Published reports from large training fleets describe multi-stage automation that classifies failures and selects the minimum viable remediation (Dubey et al. 2024; Kokolis et al. 2025; Zu et al. 2024).

The pipeline operates in four ordered stages:

  1. Health monitoring daemon: Continuously scrapes GPU telemetry (ECC error counters, temperature, fan speed, NVLink status) alongside application metrics like training loss and step throughput, often from a sidecar container.
  2. Failure classifier: Determines whether a signal indicates a fatal error (for example, NVIDIA Xid error 48: double-bit ECC error), a transient stall (for example, temporary network congestion), or a performance degradation (for example, thermal throttling).
  3. Action selector: Chooses the cheapest response consistent with the affected state and communication group. A process hang on one rank usually requires restarting the whole communication group rather than one container, because every surviving rank still holds a communicator that includes the hung rank. A confirmed hardware fault requires draining the node and drawing a replacement from the spare pool, which is slower and consumes standby capacity. A network partition can justify a bounded wait that preserves in-memory state, but if the wait expires, replacement must fence the old ownership before accepting new writes.
  4. Validation stage: Runs a recorded “canary batch” or validation workload under the expected numerical tolerance. Unexpected loss or gradient behavior triggers investigation of restored state and execution configuration, with fallback to an earlier validated snapshot when the new state cannot be trusted.

This automation removes the human page-diagnose-repair cycle from the recovery path for the common failure types, which shortens mean time to recovery. The classification stage matters most. Treating every failure as a cold restart wastes compute on transient issues, while treating a hardware failure as transient allows corrupted computation to continue. The Llama 3 pretraining run shows what such a pipeline buys at fleet scale.

War Story 7.2: The training run that failed every few hours (2024)
Context: Meta reports training Llama 3 405B on 16,384 H100 GPUs during a 54-day snapshot of pretraining (Dubey et al. 2024).

Mechanism: The snapshot recorded 419 unexpected interruptions, about one every 3.1 hours, with the root causes shown in figure 2.

Impact: Meta nevertheless reported more than 90 percent effective training time, measured as useful training time divided by elapsed time. Significant manual intervention was required only three times.

Fix: Meta reduced job startup and checkpointing time and developed automated diagnosis and recovery tools. Communication traces helped locate stalled collectives and slow workers before restarting useful work.

Systems lesson: At fleet scale, reliability is a property of the recovery system rather than of the hardware. Effective training time stayed high because automation, not operators, handled nearly every interruption.

7.9.5 Stragglers versus fail-stop failures

Not every recovery decision starts from a crashed process. When one GPU in a synchronous job slows down without dying, every healthy peer still waits at the AllReduce barrier, forcing the recovery pipeline to decide whether the slow rank is a temporary straggler or an incipient failure (perspective 7.6).

Systems Perspective 7.6: Straggler or failure
A straggler, the slow rank of a synchronous step (Synchronization models), bounds the throughput of every peer that waits on it, so one node running 10 percent slow can cost thousands of nodes 10 percent of their capacity. Fault tolerance must decide what that slowness means. A straggler can still produce correct results, so slowness alone establishes neither incorrect values nor a failed process, and its cause is often system jitter from background OS processes, network congestion, or thermal throttling rather than bad hardware. The decision is between mitigation, which keeps the job running and removes the slowdown, and recovery, which treats the rank as failed and pays for a restart.

Under the simplified no-overlap straggler model, the slowest rank sets the step time of the entire cluster because AllReduce cannot complete until every participant has submitted its gradients: \[ \StragglerStepTime(\DeviceCount) = \max(\FirstRankComputeTime, \SecondRankComputeTime, \dots, \LastRankComputeTime) + \CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount) \]

Many stragglers are gray failures, the silent degradations named in the failure taxonomy, seen from inside a synchronous job, and none of their common causes raises an explicit error. Thermal throttling reduces clock speed, degrading interconnect cables increase communication latency, OS background processes (memory scrubbing, log rotation) consume CPU cycles, and data loading from a congested network filesystem introduces variable I/O delays. A slow worker can continue meeting a liveness timeout while reducing training throughput, or become slow enough to trigger the same timeout as a failed worker. Progress measurements are therefore needed alongside liveness checks.

Timeout policy governs where the system draws the line between mitigation (redistributing work or replacing the slow node) and recovery (checkpoint-restart). Aggressive timeouts treat transient stragglers as fail-stop crashes, causing unnecessary job restarts that waste more compute than the slowdown itself, whereas conservative timeouts leave thousands of healthy GPUs waiting on a degraded node that will never speed up. The two responses also land in different places in the goodput accounting of Accounting boundaries. A slow rank that stays in the job lowers program goodput, while replacing it spends runtime goodput on the restart. The replacement decision therefore weighs one loss against the other.

Straggler mitigation strategies change either the work assignment or the execution resources. Dynamic load balancing moves work away from slow workers while preserving the intended update, whereas bounded staleness allows delayed gradients under an algorithm that tolerates them; its convergence cost depends on the workload and delay bound. Backup workers replicate selected tasks and accept one result per logical contribution, trading extra GPU cycles for a shorter wait without double-counting gradients. At the infrastructure boundary, proactive replacement uses hardware telemetry, such as rising operating temperatures or growing correctable-ECC error counts, to identify suspect components and replace them when the expected benefit exceeds migration and recovery costs. A simple cost calculation shows why a severe straggler is often cheaper to replace than to tolerate.

One straggler stalls every healthy peer at the barrier.

Napkin Math 7.4: The cost of a straggler
Consider a 1,024-GPU cluster where a normal training iteration takes 1 second. A single GPU enters a thermally throttled state, clocking down to 50 percent speed, and now takes 2 seconds to complete its computation.

Because AllReduce cannot complete until every rank has submitted its gradients, the other 1,023 healthy GPUs sit idle waiting for the straggler.

Impact:

  • Normal step time: 1 second
  • Straggler step time: 2 seconds
  • Effective cluster speed: 1 step/2s = 0.5 steps/sec

A single failing device (0.1 percent of the hardware) has reduced the throughput of the entire cluster by 50 percent. At $2/GPU-hour, this straggler wastes $1,024/hour in idle compute.

Systems insight: Replacement is worthwhile only when the expected time saved exceeds recovery and replay costs. Suppose the slowdown is expected to persist for the next \(\SlowdownPersistenceUpdateCount\) useful updates, whose step times are \(\DegradedStepTime\) on the degraded allocation and \(\HealthyStepTime\) after replacement. The simplified break-even condition is then \(\SlowdownPersistenceUpdateCount(\DegradedStepTime-\HealthyStepTime) > \JobRestartTime+ \LostWorkReplayTime\). The right side includes waiting for capacity and restoring state. A short-lived slowdown may clear before replacement pays off; false-positive detection and uncertain persistence also affect the decision. MTBF describes failure recurrence, not how long this slowdown will last.

Replacing a slow node while maintaining the original GPU count requires available capacity. An \(\DeviceCount+1\) redundancy model reserves a hot spare for the isolation and promotion stages of figure 10, reducing allocation delay. Promotion still requires compatible resources, valid state, and communication-group reconstruction; an idle device alone does not guarantee recovery within seconds. A cold spare adds startup work, while a job without a reserved spare depends on the scheduler finding capacity or on repair. The hot spare buys shorter waiting time with devices that sit idle while the fleet is healthy. Fixed-size checkpoint-restart recovery restores the original resource count. Elastic recovery relaxes that requirement by allowing the job to continue with fewer workers when the remaining allocation can support it.

Checkpoint 7.5: Tuning detection and recovery

Verify your understanding of how detection latency and recovery strategy trade against one another:

Self-Check: Question

  1. A 10,000-GPU training job experiences a GPU failure. The cluster implements cold restart, where \(T_{\text{detect}} = 30\text{ s}\), \(T_{\text{restart}} = 3\text{ min}\), \(T_{\text{load}} = 36\text{ s}\), and \(T_{\text{warmup}} = 2\text{ min}\). If the cluster MTBF is 5 hours, what is the daily compute loss incurred solely from recovery time?

    1. Approximately 0.5 minutes daily, wasting 5 GPU-hours per day.
    2. Approximately 6.6 minutes daily, wasting 100 GPU-hours per day.
    3. Approximately 15 minutes daily, wasting 500 GPU-hours per day.
    4. Approximately 29.3 minutes daily, wasting roughly 4,880 GPU-hours per day across the fleet.

  2. Compare a warm restart with a cold restart for a single-GPU failure in a 1,000-GPU cluster in terms of recovery latency, memory state guarantees, and software complexity.

  3. In a compute-dominated 1,000-GPU synchronous job, one GPU remains at half speed, reducing useful cluster throughput by approximately half. What comparison determines whether replacing it is worth the interruption?

    1. Throughput drops by only 0.1% (1/1000th); the straggler should always be tolerated to avoid restart overhead.
    2. Compare the useful work lost during replacement, restart, and replay with the work recovered by removing the slowdown over its expected remaining duration.
    3. Cluster throughput remains 100% because asynchronous gradient buffering absorbs all rank jitter.
    4. Throughput drops by 100% because any slow rank immediately triggers an NCCL assertion crash.

  4. Sequence the six sequential operational stages executed by the chapter’s stated cold-restart recovery path after classifying a hard worker failure:

  1. Resource Reclamation: Scheduler marks defective node as draining and requests spare node
  2. Job Termination: Broadcast termination signal to tear down invalidated communicator
  3. Checkpoint Loading: Workers read local partition state shards from distributed storage
  4. Training Resumption: Data loaders restore recorded position and sampling state, and training resumes
  5. Job Restart: Launch fresh container processes and re-initialize training runtime binary
  6. State Synchronization: Workers execute rendezvous and re-initialize NCCL communicators

  1. In heartbeat failure detection (\(T_{\text{timeout}} = H + k\sigma_d\)), reducing the safety multiplier \(k\) to a very small value (e.g., \(k=1\)) is universally optimal because it minimizes detection latency \(T_{\text{detect}}\) without any operational downsides.

  2. During pretraining of a foundation model, the training loss curve exhibits gradual divergence over 50 steps without any thrown exceptions or NaNs in the logs. Based on common ML failure signatures, which failure hypothesis and investigation path should be considered?

    1. Learning rate warm-up is too fast; double the learning rate and continue training.
    2. A disk full error occurred on the central coordinator; delete log files to resume execution.
    3. The tokenizer encountered an unknown character; restart training from Step 0.
    4. Silent data corruption or subtle distributed rank desynchronization may have occurred; investigate with independent replay, quarantine confirmed faulty hardware, and roll back to a known-good checkpoint prior to divergence.

See Answers →

7.10 Elastic Recovery

Suppose a 1,024-GPU training job loses an 8-GPU node to a hardware fault, but the cluster has no spare nodes available. A fixed-size recovery policy leaves the remaining 1,016 GPUs waiting until replacement capacity becomes available or the allocation is released. Elastic recovery allows the job to resize and continue with less compute, provided its state and parallel decomposition fit the smaller allocation.

Definition 7.2: Elastic training

Elastic training is the capability of a distributed training job to adjust its worker count across membership changes, potentially through a coordinated worker-group restart.

  1. Significance: It converts a permanent capacity loss into a temporary interruption followed by reduced throughput. A pure data-parallel 1,024-GPU job that loses 8 GPUs retains 99.2 percent of its device count and may resume if the remaining devices can hold the state. Tensor- and pipeline-parallel jobs additionally need a feasible partition and compatible group sizes; retained device count does not guarantee the same fraction of throughput. The recovery policy must either preserve the effective batch size through additional accumulation or validate the optimization changes caused by a smaller batch.
  2. Distinction: Unlike fixed-size checkpoint-restart recovery, which waits for replacement hardware to restore the original allocation, elastic recovery can restart or resume with a different worker count. The optimization loop may pause while membership, ranks, and state are re-established.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that elasticity is “automatic.” In reality, recovery requires coordinated adaptation: the training framework, the data loader, and the orchestrator must all synchronize their state to ensure that no data samples are lost or double-counted during the resize.

A small example shows the lifecycle in three phases (figure 16). Execution begins with a balanced four-node AllReduce ring at full throughput. When Node 3 drops out, the survivors pause at a rendezvous barrier and reload the last consistent checkpoint. The group then contracts into a three-node ring in which each worker takes a one-third larger data shard, resuming productive training at 75 percent throughput if the per-worker rate remains unchanged.

7.10.1 Recovery adaptation mechanisms

Shedding a lost node narrows the active ring instead of idling the whole allocation until replacement hardware arrives, so elastic recovery converts a job termination into a bounded capacity adjustment. The worker group may restart while the framework re-establishes membership, ranks, and state under the smaller resource allocation. When a failure reduces the worker count, the recovery path must adapt several training components before productive work resumes. Each adaptation addresses a specific consistency requirement that, if violated, would corrupt the model or waste computation.

Batch size adjustment comes first. With fewer surviving workers, either each worker must process more samples to preserve the global batch size, or the global batch size must shrink, which in turn may require recalibrating the learning rate to preserve convergence. Changing the global batch size changes the optimization regime as well as the amount of work per step. The linear and square-root learning-rate rules of Learning rate scaling rules are candidate policies for a resized job, with the worker-count ratio standing in for the batch ratio when per-worker microbatch size and accumulation count stay fixed (Goyal et al. 2017). For a shrink, the square-root rule reduces the learning rate less than linear scaling does; that arithmetic does not establish which policy is more stable, and either policy requires validation for the optimizer, schedule, and workload. Preserving the global batch instead can avoid introducing this change in optimization regime.

Goyal, Priya, Piotr Dollár, Ross Girshick, Pieter Noordhuis, Lukasz Wesolowski, Aapo Kyrola, Andrew Tulloch, Yangqing Jia, and Kaiming He. 2017. “Accurate, Large Minibatch SGD: Training ImageNet in 1 Hour.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1706.02677 abs/1706.02677.

Gradient accumulation offers that alternative. Each surviving worker processes additional micro-batches before synchronization to maintain the original effective batch size. If worker count drops by half, doubling the accumulation steps preserves the number of examples per update and can retain the existing learning-rate schedule. Each worker then performs twice as much micro-batch computation per update, although communication and overlap determine the wall-time increase. Equal batch size does not imply bitwise-identical updates or identical behavior from operations that depend on micro-batch statistics.

Data loader redistribution must agree with the recovered training step and new membership. When workers are lost, the loader reassigns the remaining logical input stream so that the resize itself neither omits samples nor counts them twice in an update. Replaying work discarded by checkpoint rollback is expected; duplicating contributions within the resumed stream is a different error. The saved data position and sampling state distinguish those cases.

State resharding adds further complexity when using sharded model parallelism (ZeRO/FSDP). When parameters and optimizer states are sharded once across the entire job without replication, losing a worker removes the only live in-memory copy of its shard, so the job must reload a complete durable checkpoint generation before repartitioning the state across the smaller worker set. When the job instead uses hybrid sharding that replicates each shard across two or more data-parallel groups, a surviving replica group still holds a live copy in GPU memory and can supply the missing shard over the network without reading from storage. Only after the missing values are recovered from a peer replica or a durable checkpoint can the runtime redistribute shard ownership across the surviving workers. Changing shard ownership alone cannot recover missing values.

Spot instance preemption as a failure class

Hardware faults are not the only event that removes workers from a running job. Cloud providers often price preemptible (“spot”) instances below on-demand capacity, with the caveat that they can be reclaimed with little warning. Once workers vanish, recovery faces the same missing-state and membership problems as a hardware failure. An advance notice, when available, can allow a coordinated save or drain before departure and therefore changes the recovery opportunity.

Both fixed-size and elastic jobs can recover from preemption using checkpoints, but a fixed-size job must regain its required allocation before restarting. An elastic job can instead resume at reduced scale if enough capacity remains and the training invariants survive the resize.
