Self-Check: Answer

  1. A single inference serving replica provides an availability of \(99\%\) (\(A_{\text{single}} = 0.99\), corresponding to 3.65 days of downtime per year). What is the theoretical annual downtime when deploying 3 independent replicas in an active-active parallel configuration (\(A_{\text{system}} = 1 - (1 - A_{\text{single}})^3\)), and why is real-world availability typically lower?

    1. Annual downtime is 52.6 minutes; real-world availability is lower because load balancers introduce \(10\%\) packet loss.
    2. Annual downtime is 3.65 days; real-world availability is unchanged because adding replicas does not change individual failure rates.
    3. Annual downtime is 0 seconds; three replicas provide mathematically absolute fault tolerance under all operating conditions.
    4. Annual downtime drops to approximately 31.5 seconds (\(99.9999\%\) availability); real-world availability is lower because correlated failures (shared power, top-of-rack switches, DNS, software bugs) violate the independence assumption.

    Answer: The correct answer is D. The formula for \(k=3\) independent replicas yields \(A_{\text{system}} = 1 - (1 - 0.99)^3 = 1 - (0.01)^3 = 1 - 10^{-6} = 0.999999\) (\(99.9999\%\) availability, or \(365 \times 24 \times 3600 \times 10^{-6} \approx 31.5\) seconds downtime/year). In practice, shared failure domains (such as common top-of-rack switches, power feeds, shared feature stores, or buggy model code deployed across all replicas) introduce correlation that prevents achieving six-nines availability. Two independent 99-percent replicas yield about 52.6 minutes of annual downtime, whereas three yield about 31.5 seconds. The 3.65-day option ignores redundancy entirely. Absolute zero downtime is physically impossible. This idealized formula also assumes reliable routing and enough ready capacity on the surviving replicas to serve demand; independence alone does not establish request-level availability.

    Learning Objective: Calculate redundant replica availability and identify factors that violate component independence.

  2. Explain why losing the key-value (KV) cache of an active 128K-token conversation during a GPU failure in stateful LLM serving severely degrades user latency, and describe how prefix caching mitigates this impact.

    Answer: The replacement replica must reconstruct any lost per-session KV state before continuing generation, adding prefill latency. A surviving replica with a matching cached prefix can avoid recomputing that prefix, but the uncached conversation suffix still needs processing. Prefix caching helps only when reusable state remains available; it does not automatically replicate every session cache or eliminate failover latency.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the latency and state preservation challenges of transformer KV caches in stateful inference serving.

  3. What is the primary operational trade-off between Active-Active and Active-Passive replication strategies in ML inference serving clusters?

    1. Active-Active replication requires \(100\%\) manual failover by human operators, whereas Active-Passive replication is fully automated.
    2. Active-Active distributes traffic across all live replicas to maximize resource utilization but requires reserve headroom to absorb traffic when a replica fails; Active-Passive maintains synchronized standby replicas that sit idle during normal operation, simplifying failover at higher resource cost.
    3. Active-Active replication can only be deployed on CPU clusters, whereas Active-Passive replication is exclusive to GPUs.
    4. Active-Active replication guarantees zero latency during network partitions, whereas Active-Passive replication triples inference compute requirements.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. In Active-Active serving, all replicas actively process incoming requests, maximizing infrastructure efficiency, but the cluster must operate below peak capacity (with headroom) so surviving replicas are not overwhelmed when one fails. In Active-Passive serving, dedicated standby replicas remain idle while tracking state or heartbeats, ensuring clean failover capacity at the cost of paying for idle hardware. The other choices mischaracterize automation, hardware support, and network partition physics.

    Learning Objective: Compare active-active and active-passive replication strategies for model inference workloads.

  4. In stateless model serving (such as single-image classification), a crashed GPU replica requires complex distributed state synchronization and session rollback before a client’s request can be retried on another node.

    Answer: False. A stateless request supplies the input needed by another compatible, ready replica, so no session-state restoration is required. A retry still needs surviving capacity, safe operation semantics, the original deadline and a bounded retry budget. Routing or detection delays can remain; statelessness does not guarantee immediate successful completion.

    Learning Objective: Compare request failover mechanisms between stateless and stateful model serving systems.

  5. After a serving replica suffers a GPU fault, what dependencies must hold before requests can safely use alternate replicas, and which recovery work can proceed concurrently?

    Answer: Health evidence must remove the failed replica from routing. Alternate replicas must be ready and have capacity; stateful requests also need their required context restored or reconstructed. Retried work must be safe, remain within its original deadline and retry budget, and pass admission control. Provisioning a replacement can proceed concurrently with rerouting to existing ready replicas. Detection, routing propagation and state recovery can add delay; failover is not necessarily seamless.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the automated health detection and traffic rerouting stages during inference replica failover.

  6. Compare session affinity with external distributed state stores (such as Redis) for managing stateful conversational inference sessions in terms of failover speed and infrastructure complexity.

    Answer: Session affinity keeps a conversation on the replica holding its KV cache, avoiding remote state access but exposing that session to cache loss when the replica fails. An external store can preserve conversation context so another replica can recover it. Unless KV state was also preserved, the replacement still recomputes the cache. The trade-off includes state-transfer latency and another storage dependency.

    Learning Objective: Compare session affinity against distributed state stores for stateful inference fault tolerance.

  1. A serving system under overload must drop some requests. Why does priority-based load shedding generally serve the product better than random shedding?

    1. Random shedding drops more total requests than priority-based shedding at the same load.
    2. Priority-based shedding eliminates the need for admission control at the system entry point.
    3. Random shedding treats every request as equally valuable, while priority-based shedding degrades according to a predeclared contract, serving interactive and revenue-generating requests before background or analytics work.
    4. Priority-based shedding guarantees that no request misses its latency target during overload.

    Answer: The correct answer is C. Both policies preserve capacity by dropping a fraction of demand, so neither inherently drops fewer requests. Random shedding ignores request value, whereas priority-based shedding turns degradation into an explicit policy that protects the most valuable traffic first. Admission control is still needed to reject work at entry, and no shedding policy guarantees every surviving request meets its target.

    Learning Objective: Compare load-shedding policies by how they allocate unavoidable service loss across request classes.

  2. Explain the four-tier feature degradation strategy (Critical, Important, Useful, Optional) and describe how it maintains service availability when feature store dependencies degrade.

    Answer: The example policy blocks requests missing Critical features, substitutes validated defaults for Important features, uses cached values for Useful features, and omits Optional features. Its 5–10%, 2–5%, and less-than-2% quality-loss ranges are illustrative policy budgets, not inherent effects of those categories. Defaults and cached representations must be compatible with the model, and measured fallback quality must satisfy the chosen budget before deployment.

    Learning Objective: Design tiered feature degradation policies to maintain inference service availability during feature store outages.

  3. An image classification service deploys a model cascade: ViT-Large (307M params, primary, 88% acc), EfficientNet-B4 (19M params, secondary, 83% acc), and MobileNet-V3 (5.4M params, tertiary, 75% acc). How should the serving tier execute model fallback during severe traffic spikes or partial accelerator outages?

    1. It dynamically routes incoming requests to lighter secondary and tertiary models based on queue depth and p99 latency triggers with hysteresis, maintaining low latency and high availability at measured accuracy cost.
    2. It executes all three models simultaneously on every request and votes on the majority class.
    3. It drops all incoming traffic until additional ViT-Large GPU replicas finish provisioning.
    4. It converts the ViT-Large model into a text-only classifier to save memory.

    Answer: The correct answer is A. Model fallback ladders dynamically shift traffic to lighter, lower-compute models when queue depths or tail latency violate SLAs, preserving response availability while sacrificing a bounded percentage of accuracy. Hysteresis ensures the system does not oscillate rapidly between tiers. Executing all three adds their unequal compute costs rather than reducing load. Dropping traffic violates availability goals. Converting vision models to text-only is nonsensical.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate multi-tier model fallback cascades for graceful quality-latency trade-offs.

  4. Graceful degradation is an automatic emergent property of standard microservice architectures that requires no explicit pre-engineering or offline fallback validation.

    Answer: False. Graceful degradation requires extensive pre-engineering: fallback models must be pre-deployed or precomputed, default feature stores populated, degradation triggers (latency, error rate) instrumented, fallback accuracy validated against reduced SLOs, and hysteresis policies implemented to prevent oscillation.

    Learning Objective: Justify the requirement for proactive architectural design and validation in graceful degradation systems.

  5. The fault-tolerance strategy of selectively dropping lower-priority background requests during severe load spikes to protect latency SLAs for high-priority interactive requests is known as load ____.

    Answer: The correct answer is shedding (or load shedding / priority load shedding). Load shedding protects system stability by proactively dropping a portion of demand before saturation causes cascading timeouts.

    Learning Objective: Explain load shedding strategies used to protect serving availability under extreme resource saturation.

Self-Check: Answer

  1. Explain why Heisenbugs are particularly pervasive and challenging to diagnose in distributed collective communication (e.g., NCCL AllReduce) libraries.

    Answer: Heisenbugs occur when the act of observing or instrumenting a software system alters its timing and masks the underlying defect. In distributed collective communication, inserting detailed logging, tracing hooks, or debug barriers alters packet transmission timing and synchronization barriers across ranks, inadvertently resolving the precise race condition, thread interleaving, or network jitter that triggered the deadlock or corruption in production.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the diagnostic challenges posed by Heisenbugs and timing perturbations in distributed collective libraries.

  2. In a production ML recommendation service, p99 latency suddenly jumps from 45 ms to 800 ms with a 15% error rate. How do the three pillars of observability (Metrics, Traces, Logs) work together to diagnose the root cause?

    1. Metrics reveal that GPU utilization is 100%; Traces show that all requests are identical; Logs show that user passwords were typed incorrectly.
    2. Metrics reveal the exact timing and scope of the latency spike; distributed Traces isolate the feature-service span consuming 700 ms (instead of normal 12 ms); structured Logs reveal repeated connection timeouts to the embedding cache followed by a database miss storm.
    3. Traces generate automatic pull requests to fix the bug; Metrics execute the rollback; Logs alert the end users.
    4. The three pillars cannot be correlated because each uses completely incompatible timestamp formats.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Metrics identify the symptom’s timing and scope; traces locate the excessive delay in the feature-service span; logs connect cache timeouts with increased direct database requests. Together they narrow the failure path and support containment through a circuit breaker and validated fallback. They do not yet distinguish a failed cache process from a network or resource problem, so targeted repair requires corroborating evidence. The other choices misstate the functions of observability or deny the shared identifiers that allow correlation.

    Learning Objective: Analyze metrics, distributed traces, and structured logs to diagnose cascade failures in ML pipelines.

  3. Explain the numerical mechanism of NaN propagation during backward gradient computation in FP16 mixed-precision training, and describe how loss scaling prevents gradient underflow.

    Answer: Invalid operations such as zero divided by zero can produce NaN, which propagates through most arithmetic into gradients and optimizer state. Overflow can first produce infinity and later cause NaN through an invalid combination. Loss scaling raises small FP16 gradients above the underflow range, then unscales them before the update. Dynamic scaling must also detect nonfinite gradients and skip invalid updates; scaling alone does not cure arbitrary NaNs.

    Learning Objective: Analyze numerical debugging techniques and loss scaling mechanisms in mixed-precision training.

  4. In distributed tracing systems utilizing OpenTelemetry, capturing execution time across microservices is sufficient for incident triage without recording structured trace and span correlation identifiers in service logs.

    Answer: False as a general sufficiency claim. Trace timing helps locate delayed spans, while trace and span identifiers in structured logs link those spans to local events and exceptions. Other evidence may still support initial triage without that linkage, but missing correlation makes exact request-level diagnosis harder.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the role of trace and span context propagation in distributed log correlation.

Self-Check: Answer

  1. Why might a software-based fault injection framework (such as PyTorchFI) overestimate a neural network’s vulnerability to radiation-induced soft errors when compared against physical beam testing on real silicon?

    1. Software-based tools only inject faults into 8-bit integer quantized models, whereas physical radiation only affects 64-bit floating point registers.
    2. Physical radiation only damages power delivery networks and never alters logic gate states in operational GPUs.
    3. Software injection tools can only simulate permanent stuck-at faults and cannot model transient bit flips.
    4. Software injection mutates high-level tensor variables directly, completely bypassing microarchitectural and circuit-level masking (such as ECC correction, speculative instruction discarding, and dead registers) that absorb soft errors in hardware.

    Answer: The correct answer is D. In physical hardware, many single-event upsets are masked by circuit-level defenses (ECC, parity checks) or microarchitectural factors (errors on unused registers, speculative instructions that get squashed, or overwritten memory addresses before reads occur). Software-level fault injection bypasses these hardware filtering stages and directly perturbs active software tensors, leading to higher observed failure rates than physical beam experiments. The other options misstate data types, radiation physics, and the capabilities of software tools.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the abstraction gap between software-based fault injection and physical hardware fault mechanisms.

  2. Explain why killing a rank mid-collective, partitioning a node from the rendezvous service, and corrupting one checkpoint shard are more valuable fleet-recovery experiments than tensor bit flips alone.

    Answer: Each experiment exercises a recovery decision rather than only a corrupted value. Killing a rank or delaying its heartbeat tests whether detection timeouts fire and the worker group is rebuilt; partitioning a node from rendezvous tests whether membership and fencing stop the old owner from writing; corrupting a shard tests whether restore rejects a generation whose manifest digests do not match. A tensor bit flip tests how software responds to a wrong value, but it does not show that the detector fires before the protected commit or that recovery stays within its lost-work and recovery-time bounds.

    Learning Objective: Design fault-injection experiments that exercise recovery decisions at the level where they are made.

  3. Software-based fault injection frameworks are universally preferred over FPGA-based injection for production resilience certification because software tools capture register-level gate timing violations and physical alpha-particle strikes with higher fidelity.

    Answer: False. Software injection trades physical fidelity for scale and controllable experiments. FPGA-based injection can expose lower-level state in the implemented design, while beam testing probes the actual device under physical radiation. Neither an FPGA implementation nor a tensor-level simulation automatically reproduces every timing, packaging, or fault-propagation behavior of production silicon.

    Learning Objective: Compare the fidelity and scalability trade-offs between hardware-based and software-based fault injection methodologies.

  4. Explain how fault-injection frameworks such as Fidelity bridge the abstraction gap between low-level hardware faults and high-level software models.

    Answer: Such frameworks model how lower-level faults in registers, memory and numerical units become software-visible errors, then use that mapping to guide scalable software injection. The mapping narrows the abstraction gap; its fidelity depends on the modeled hardware, fault distribution and calibration. It does not automatically establish coverage of every production fault.

    Learning Objective: Analyze how bridging frameworks map microarchitectural hardware faults to software-level tensor perturbations.

Self-Check: Answer

  1. Why does Google’s TPUv4 recovery design combine OCS-based replacement of unhealthy cubes with fault-tolerant routing for supported link-outage patterns?

    1. Because TPU chips have zero local memory and cannot execute software routing algorithms.
    2. Because optical circuit switches are cheaper than standard copper Ethernet cables for intra-rack links.
    3. A machine outage can remove a required job participant, requiring a healthy replacement cube; a link outage can instead require a supported alternate communication path. The response must preserve the job’s communication topology.
    4. Because OCS switches completely eliminate the need for checkpointing during multi-month training jobs.

    Answer: The correct answer is C. OCS allows the recovery system to assemble job-sized slices from healthy spare cubes after machine outages. Fault-tolerant routing handles supported OCS outage patterns when alternate paths remain available. Replacing a participant and repairing a communication path are distinct responses; neither follows from the failed-device count alone. The other choices misrepresent memory architecture or economics, or incorrectly eliminate checkpointing.

    Learning Objective: Analyze how optical circuit switching enables topology-preserving fault tolerance in tightly coupled accelerator pods.

  2. During the training of Meta’s OPT-175B model on 992 A100 GPUs, describe how the team addressed both physical hardware failures and mathematical loss spikes.

    Answer: For physical hardware failures (at least 35 manual and an estimated 70+ automatic restarts over two months from ECC memory errors, network issues, and lost GPUs), the team relied on restart from the latest checkpoint, with train-log freshness monitoring (a 15-minute threshold, later relaxed to one hour) contributing to detection delay; polling and response could extend the idle window. For mathematical loss spikes (numerical divergence and gradient overflow), the team rolled back to an earlier clean checkpoint and resumed with a reduced learning rate (ultimately roughly two-thirds of GPT-3’s rate). The two remedies differ, which is why the run had to retain checkpoint history rather than only its newest snapshot.

    Learning Objective: Compare physical hardware fault recovery and algorithmic numerical divergence recovery in large-scale LLM training.

  3. What fundamental principle of ML serving resilience is demonstrated by Netflix’s chaos engineering practice?

    1. Resilience is an active practice of continuous verification: intentionally injecting latency, terminating feature caches, and simulating dependency outages under controlled conditions to empirically verify that fallback hierarchies function before real outages occur.
    2. Fault tolerance code should only be executed during unplanned production disasters to minimize server load.
    3. Machine learning models should never rely on external feature stores or databases.
    4. Chaos testing is only applicable to CPU-based web servers and cannot be used in GPU inference clusters.

    Answer: The correct answer is A. Netflix’s chaos engineering principles establish that fault tolerance mechanisms (such as model fallbacks, circuit breakers, and feature defaults) rot if left unexercised. Regularly injecting synthetic faults in production and staging verifies that fallback paths actually satisfy latency and availability SLOs when real dependencies fail. The other options contradict chaos engineering principles or assert false restrictions on ML systems.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the role of chaos engineering and active failure injection in validating ML serving fallback paths.

  4. Google’s TPUv4 study reports daily failure rates of 0.08 percent of TPU machines, 0.005 percent of ICI cables, and 0.04 percent of OCS units. Explain why such small daily fractions still require an automated reconfiguration control plane rather than manual repair.

    Answer: Small daily fractions multiply across thousands of machines, cables, and switches, so the pod meets a steady stream of failures rather than an occasional emergency. Each failure can interrupt a job that needs its slice’s full communication topology, so the response must assemble a healthy slice from spare cubes or route around a failed link quickly and repeatedly. Health monitoring and topology software that run this loop automatically keep availability high; a manual repair process would leave jobs waiting for every routine event.

    Learning Objective: Analyze how fleet-scale failure statistics justify automated topology reconfiguration.

Self-Check: Answer

  1. An engineering team operates a 1,000-GPU training cluster with a system MTBF of 50 hours (\(3{,}000\text{ minutes}\)) and a checkpoint write time of 5 minutes. The team intuitively chooses to checkpoint ‘every 15 minutes to stay safe.’ According to the Young-Daly formula, what is the mathematically optimal interval, and what is the consequence of the team’s intuitive choice?

    1. Optimal interval is 15 minutes; the intuitive choice is mathematically perfect.
    2. The first-order optimum is approximately 173 minutes (~2.9 hours); writing every 15 minutes adds more checkpoint overhead than it saves in expected rework under this model.
    3. Optimal interval is 50 hours; checkpointing every 15 minutes causes disk sectors to wear out in days.
    4. Optimal interval is 1 minute; checkpointing every 15 minutes risks losing 100% of the training dataset.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Applying the Young-Daly formula: \(\tau_{\text{opt}} = \sqrt{2 \cdot T_{\text{write}} \cdot \text{MTBF}_{\text{system}}} = \sqrt{2 \times 5 \times 3000} = \sqrt{30{,}000} \approx 173.2\text{ minutes}\) (approx. 2.9 hours). Setting an interval of 15 minutes deviates by more than an order of magnitude, spending massive cluster time on unnecessary storage writes. The 15-minute, 50-hour, and 1-minute options misapply the square-root law or make exaggerated failure claims. A stricter recovery point objective can justify a shorter interval even when it increases expected waste.

    Learning Objective: Calculate Young-Daly optimal intervals to demonstrate why intuition-based checkpoint scheduling causes excessive I/O overhead.

  2. Explain what a GPU-only independent-failure estimate, \(\mathrm{MTBF}_{\mathrm{system}}=\mathrm{MTBF}_{\mathrm{component}}/N\), omits about shared failure domains and their recovery consequences.

    Answer: The independent-component formula omits separate shared failure modes such as power or switch outages, and it says nothing about their blast radius. Add those fault domains and their recovery consequences explicitly. Correlation alone does not necessarily increase the count of distinct incidents for fixed marginal component rates; a shared outage can group many component failures into one large event.

    Learning Objective: Justify why independent component reliability models fail to capture the risk and blast radius of correlated infrastructure failures.

  3. Why does adding more GPUs (\(N\)) to a distributed training job eventually encounter a ‘reliability ceiling’ where total wall-clock training time increases rather than decreases?

    1. Because GPUs consume more electrical power than data center transformers can supply.
    2. Because floating-point numbers lose precision when divided across more than 1,000 workers.
    3. Because the Python interpreter cannot spawn more than 512 concurrent threads.
    4. Because aggregate cluster failure rate scales linearly (\(N\lambda\)), shrinking system MTBF until the cumulative time lost to failure detection, job restarts, checkpoint loading, and rework exceeds the marginal compute speedup of additional parallelism.

    Answer: The correct answer is D. Under independent failures, adding workers increases the aggregate failure rate. Recovery and replay can eventually cost more than the marginal compute speedup, and this reliability ceiling applies on top of the Amdahl ceiling. Whole-run useful goodput must count only retained progress and include recovery time in the denominator, so its reliability penalty grows with the number of workers.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the reliability ceiling where aggregate cluster failure overhead outpaces parallel compute scaling.

  4. Because modern accelerator hardware incorporates hardware ECC on High Bandwidth Memory and CRC on interconnects, application-level gradient checksums and loss validation checks are redundant and unnecessary in 10,000-GPU clusters.

    Answer: False. ECC and CRC protect the error patterns covered by their implementations within storage and transport boundaries. They do not validate arbitrary arithmetic or software transformations. Checksums test byte integrity or agreement, while replay, independent computation, invariants and statistical checks provide different evidence about computed results. Coverage must match the declared fault model; no single check establishes end-to-end correctness.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the limitations of hardware ECC and justify the necessity of end-to-end software verification against silent data corruption.
